Is it ethical to expose children to systems whose long-term cognitive and emotional effects are unknown?

# The Ethics of Uncertainty: Children, AI, and the Burden of the Unknown

Every generation of children has been the first to grow up with some new technology whose full consequences were not yet understood. What distinguishes the current moment is not novelty alone but the particular intimacy of the systems involved. AI tutors, conversational agents, and adaptive learning platforms do not merely occupy children's attention; they respond to them, model their behaviour, infer their emotional states, and shape the cognitive habits through which they will approach learning for the rest of their lives. The ethical question this raises is not simply whether AI in education is good or bad, but whether it is justifiable to deploy systems of such developmental consequence before their long-term effects are known.

The precautionary principle offers the most direct framework for thinking through this problem. Originating in environmental and bioethics discourse, it holds that when an action raises credible threats of harm to a vulnerable population, protective measures are warranted even in the absence of complete scientific certainty. Children are recognised across multiple ethical and legal traditions as a uniquely vulnerable group, not merely because they are small or dependent, but because they are in the midst of forming the cognitive architectures, emotional regulation capacities, and social dispositions that will define their adult lives. Interventions during these developmental windows carry asymmetric risk: harms that might be trivial or reversible in adults can become structurally embedded in children. This is the core of the precautionary argument — not that AI is demonstrably harmful, but that the stakes of being wrong are unusually high.

Yet the precautionary principle is not self-executing, and its application to AI in education is genuinely contested. Critics of strong precautionary positions point out that withholding technology also carries costs. Children in under-resourced schools who might benefit from adaptive instruction, personalised feedback, or AI-assisted literacy support are not protected by abstention — they are simply denied a potential advantage. The ethical calculus therefore cannot treat deployment and non-deployment as asymmetric options where only the former carries risk. Inaction has its own developmental consequences, and these fall disproportionately on children who already face educational disadvantage. This tension between the risk of harm and the risk of deprivation is not easily resolved, and any honest ethical analysis must hold both sides in view.

What makes the uncertainty particularly difficult to navigate is its character. The unknown effects of AI on children are not merely gaps waiting to be filled by more research; they are in some respects structurally unknowable in advance, because the systems are novel, because children's development is complex and variable, and because the relevant timescales — effects on adult cognition, emotional resilience, or social capacity — extend far beyond any feasible trial period. This is different from, say, a pharmaceutical whose side effects can be measured in a controlled study. The cognitive and emotional effects of sustained interaction with responsive AI systems are entangled with everything else in a child's environment, making clean causal attribution extremely difficult even retrospectively.

This epistemic situation places particular weight on the question of consent and governance. Children cannot meaningfully consent to participation in what is, in effect, a large-scale developmental experiment. Their parents can consent on their behalf, but parental consent is only ethically adequate when parents have genuine access to relevant information — which, given the novelty of these systems and the opacity of their mechanisms, is rarely the case. Institutional and regulatory frameworks therefore carry a burden that individual consent cannot discharge. The ethical deployment of AI in educational settings requires not just parental permission forms but robust oversight structures, mandatory longitudinal monitoring, transparent reporting of adverse signals, and genuine mechanisms for withdrawal and redress.

There is also a distributional dimension that ethical analysis cannot ignore. The children most likely to be exposed to AI educational systems at scale — through public school procurement decisions, government-funded programmes, or resource-constrained institutions seeking efficiency — are often not the same children whose parents have the social capital to scrutinise those systems, opt out, or advocate for alternatives. This means that the burden of developmental uncertainty is not shared equally. It tends to fall on children who are already less protected by other social institutions, which compounds rather than mitigates the ethical concern.

None of this amounts to a categorical prohibition. It does amount to a serious obligation. The ethical deployment of AI in children's education requires treating uncertainty not as a temporary inconvenience to be managed with disclaimers, but as a substantive moral fact that demands ongoing vigilance, genuine accountability, and a willingness to slow or reverse deployment when evidence of harm emerges. The question is not whether children should ever encounter AI systems whose effects are incompletely understood — in a world of rapid technological change, that condition is unavoidable. The question is whether the institutions responsible for children's welfare are taking that uncertainty seriously enough.

What consent can a minor meaningfully give to AI-mediated education?

## What Consent Can a Minor Meaningfully Give to AI-Mediated Education?

The question of what consent a minor can meaningfully give to AI-mediated education sits at the intersection of developmental psychology, legal architecture, and the particular opacity of algorithmic systems. It is a question that existing frameworks answer only partially, and often in ways that were designed for a simpler technological era.

The primary legal architecture governing minors' consent in AI-mediated education in the United States rests on two federal statutes: the Children's Online Privacy Protection Act (COPPA, 1998, updated 2013) and the Family Educational Rights and Privacy Act (FERPA, 1974). COPPA prohibits the collection of personal data from children under 13 without verifiable parental consent, and the Federal Trade Commission has enforced this standard against edtech providers with increasing seriousness. FERPA, meanwhile, grants parents rights over their children's educational records and transfers those rights to students themselves once they reach eighteen. Between these two poles lies a significant grey zone: adolescents aged thirteen to seventeen occupy a legal space where neither robust parental gatekeeping nor full autonomous consent is clearly mandated. The European Union's GDPR, and its child-specific provisions sometimes called GDPR-K, set the digital age of consent between thirteen and sixteen depending on member state, and require that any consent obtained from a child be given in language genuinely comprehensible to that child. These frameworks establish a floor, not a ceiling, and they were largely designed before adaptive AI tutoring systems, emotion-recognition software, and large language model-based educational tools became widespread.

The legal question and the philosophical question diverge sharply here. Law tends to treat consent as a binary threshold crossed at a particular age, but developmental research presents a more graduated picture. Cognitive capacity for understanding abstract consequences, evaluating probabilistic risk, and resisting social pressure develops unevenly across adolescence and is not reliably complete until the early twenties. A fourteen-year-old may be capable of assenting to a tutoring platform's stated terms while being entirely unable to reason about what it means for that platform to build a longitudinal behavioral profile, sell inferred emotional states to third parties, or use interaction data to train future models. The gap between surface comprehension and genuine informed consent is not a failure of individual intelligence; it reflects the inherent complexity of modern AI data practices, which confound many adults as well.

This complexity introduces a structural problem that no age threshold resolves. Meaningful consent requires, at minimum, that the consenting party understand what they are agreeing to, that they have a genuine alternative, and that they are free from undue pressure. AI-mediated education increasingly fails all three conditions for minors. The technical disclosures in privacy policies are written at reading levels far above those of the students they nominally inform. Where AI tools are embedded in compulsory schooling, the alternative to consent is effectively exclusion from education itself, which is not a free choice. And the social dynamics of school environments, combined with the persuasive design features common to edtech platforms, create conditions that researchers in behavioral science would recognize as structurally coercive, even when no individual actor intends coercion.

There is genuine debate about how to respond to this situation. One position holds that because minors cannot meaningfully consent, the burden should fall entirely on institutions and parents, with students' assent treated as a courtesy rather than a legal foundation. This view has the advantage of honesty about the limits of minor autonomy, but critics argue it risks treating young people as passive objects of decisions made entirely by others, which itself carries developmental and ethical costs. An alternative position, gaining traction in children's rights scholarship, holds that the goal should be graduated, supported consent: age-appropriate explanation, genuine opt-out mechanisms that do not penalize students academically, and increasing participation in consent decisions as students mature. The UK's Age Appropriate Design Code, which requires platforms to consider children's best interests by default rather than waiting for consent to fail, represents a regulatory attempt to move in this direction.

What remains contested is whether any consent framework is adequate to the specific features of AI systems in education. Unlike a static database, an adaptive AI system changes its behavior based on accumulated inferences about a student, inferences that may concern not just academic performance but attention patterns, emotional states, and social dynamics. The student who consented at enrollment could not have consented to the profile that emerges two years later, because that profile did not yet exist. This temporal problem suggests that point-of-entry consent, however carefully designed, is structurally insufficient for AI-mediated education, and that ongoing, revisable, and genuinely informed engagement with data practices may be the only ethically coherent standard — one that current law does not yet require and that current edtech design rarely supports.

How do we preserve academic integrity when the boundary between assistance and authorship dissolves?

## The Dissolution of Authorship: Academic Integrity in the Age of Generative AI

The question of what constitutes genuine academic work has never been as philosophically clean as institutions pretended. Students have always used tutors, writing centres, and collaborative discussion; the line between being helped to think and having someone else think for you was always drawn by convention as much as by principle. What large language models have done is not create this ambiguity but make it operationally impossible to ignore. When a student asks an AI to brainstorm, then to outline, then to draft, then to revise, and finally submits the result under their own name, the question of who authored the work resists any simple answer — and the consequences of getting that answer wrong fall on students, educators, and the credibility of qualifications alike.

The scale of the problem became undeniable with Turnitin's 2023 report, which identified potential AI-generated content in over ten million submitted papers within the first year of its detection tool's deployment. That figure does not distinguish between wholesale generation and minor AI-assisted editing, which is precisely the difficulty. The traditional binary — legitimate assistance on one side, academic dishonesty on the other — was already philosophically unstable before generative AI arrived. A student who dictates ideas to a more fluent friend occupies an uncomfortable middle ground that most honour codes simply declined to address. Large language models have forced that evasion to end, because the middle ground is now where most students live.

The core conceptual problem is what researchers have called the "assistance spectrum." At one end sits a student who uses an AI tool to check grammar; at the other sits one who submits a prompt response verbatim. Between these poles lies an enormous range of practices — using AI to overcome writer's block, to generate counterarguments the student then evaluates, to restructure a draft the student wrote — none of which maps neatly onto existing categories of cheating. Institutions that respond by banning AI use entirely are, in effect, banning a technology their students will be expected to use professionally within years of graduating. Institutions that permit unrestricted use risk certifying competencies students have not demonstrated. Neither extreme is defensible.

What the evidence suggests is that the integrity question cannot be separated from the learning question. If an assessment is designed to measure a student's capacity to construct an argument, and an AI constructs it instead, the assessment has failed regardless of whether the student technically violated a policy. This reframes the problem productively: the goal is not primarily to catch cheaters but to design conditions under which the work submitted genuinely reflects the student's intellectual development. Process-based assessment — requiring drafts, reflective annotations, oral defences, or documented revision histories — makes AI substitution harder and, more importantly, makes the learning visible in ways a single polished product cannot. Several universities have moved toward "AI use statements" modelled on existing disclosure norms for research assistance, requiring students to document what tools they used and how. This approach treats transparency as the operative value rather than prohibition, which is both more honest about the technology's ubiquity and more educationally coherent.

Detection technology offers only partial relief, and institutions that rely on it heavily are likely to be disappointed. AI-detection tools, including Turnitin's own, operate probabilistically and carry meaningful false-positive rates — a particular concern for non-native English speakers, whose writing can share statistical features with AI-generated text. Treating a detection flag as proof of misconduct is not only procedurally unjust but likely to produce discriminatory outcomes. The tools are also in an arms race with generation models that are rapidly improving at producing text that evades detection. Detection can serve as one signal among many in a holistic review, but it cannot bear the evidentiary weight that some administrators have placed on it.

The deeper tension in this debate is between two legitimate values: the integrity of credentials as meaningful signals of individual competence, and the recognition that all intellectual work is collaborative and tool-mediated. A student who uses a calculator is not cheating at mathematics; a student who uses a spell-checker is not misrepresenting their literacy. Whether AI assistance is more like a calculator or more like a ghostwriter depends entirely on what the assessment is trying to measure — and that is a question institutions have often left unanswered. Making it explicit is the necessary first step.

Preserving academic integrity in this environment requires, above all, clarity of purpose. Assessments should be designed around learning outcomes that AI cannot simply discharge on a student's behalf, or that require demonstrated understanding AI cannot fake in a live exchange. Policies should distinguish between uses of AI that support learning and uses that replace it. And the standard of honesty owed to students demands acknowledging that this distinction, while real, will always require judgment rather than algorithm to enforce.

Should there be a "right to a human teacher," analogous to a right to human medical care?

## The Right to a Human Teacher: Education, Dignity, and the Limits of Artificial Intelligence

The rapid deployment of artificial intelligence in classrooms has revived a question that might once have seemed merely theoretical: does every learner possess a right not merely to education, but specifically to a human educator? The analogy to medical care is instructive. Most liberal democracies, whatever their disagreements about healthcare financing, accept that patients facing serious clinical decisions deserve access to a human professional — someone capable of moral judgment, empathic response, and genuine accountability. Whether an equivalent principle applies to teaching depends on what education is fundamentally understood to be.

Existing international human rights instruments suggest that the answer cannot be reduced to a question of instructional efficiency. The UN Convention on the Rights of the Child, particularly Article 29, and UNESCO's broader frameworks on the right to education do not define that right merely as access to information or the acquisition of measurable skills. They frame education as the holistic development of the child's personality, dignity, and social capacities. Legal scholars such as Fons Coomans have argued on this basis that the right to education is inherently relational — that it presupposes encounters between persons, not merely between a learner and a content-delivery system. On this reading, the philosophical foundations for something like a right to a human teacher are already latent within existing law, even if they have never been articulated in those terms.

The medical analogy illuminates both the strength and the limits of this argument. In healthcare, the case for human involvement rests on several distinct grounds: the complexity of clinical judgment, the moral weight of decisions about a person's body, the therapeutic value of the patient-physician relationship itself, and the need for a human agent who can be held legally and ethically accountable. Each of these has a plausible educational counterpart. Teachers exercise judgment that goes well beyond content delivery — they read emotional states, navigate social dynamics, make real-time decisions about when to challenge and when to support, and model the very intellectual and ethical dispositions they are trying to cultivate. These are not incidental features of good teaching; on most serious accounts of education, they are constitutive of it.

Yet the analogy also has limits that honest analysis must acknowledge. Medicine involves interventions on the body with direct risks of physical harm, which creates a particularly strong case for human oversight and accountability. The harms of AI-mediated education, while real and increasingly documented — including the erosion of social development, the reinforcement of algorithmic bias, and the attenuation of the mentorship relationship — are more diffuse and harder to attribute causally. This makes the legal and political case for a formal right more difficult to construct, even if the philosophical case remains compelling.

There is also a tension within the progressive case for such a right that deserves acknowledgment. In many parts of the world, the alternative to AI-assisted instruction is not a well-trained, attentive human teacher but an overcrowded classroom with an undertrained and under-resourced one. For learners in those circumstances, AI tools may represent a genuine improvement in the quality of educational attention they receive. To insist on a right to human teaching without simultaneously insisting on the conditions that make human teaching genuinely valuable risks becoming a formal right that protects an inadequate status quo. The right, if it is to mean anything, must be a right to human teaching of a certain quality — which immediately raises questions about resourcing, training, and political will that go far beyond the technology debate.

What seems more defensible than an absolute right is a principle of human primacy: that AI systems in education should be positioned as tools that support and extend human teachers rather than replace them, and that learners should retain meaningful access to human educators for the relational, developmental, and accountability functions that AI cannot discharge. This is closer to how the medical analogy actually operates in practice — AI diagnostic tools are increasingly used in medicine, but the right to a human physician for consequential decisions remains intact.

The emerging consensus among education researchers and legal theorists, though it remains contested, leans toward this intermediate position. A categorical right to a human teacher in every instructional moment is probably neither enforceable nor, in all contexts, desirable. But the underlying intuition driving the question — that education is a fundamentally human relationship, that children's development cannot be fully entrusted to systems incapable of genuine moral engagement, and that accountability requires human agents — is grounded in the same dignity-based reasoning that underlies rights to human medical care. The question is not whether that intuition deserves legal expression, but how precisely to express it without either dismissing the genuine benefits of AI or surrendering the relational core of what education has always been.

How do we address the cultural and ideological biases embedded in models trained predominantly on certain languages and worldviews?

## Cultural and Ideological Bias in AI Educational Tools: Origins, Consequences, and Remedies

The dominant large language models shaping contemporary educational technology were built, in large part, from the English-language internet. That seemingly technical fact carries profound cultural consequences. As Bender and colleagues documented in their influential 2021 "Stochastic Parrots" analysis, the Common Crawl dataset — a foundational corpus for many major models — disproportionately reflects content produced by English-speaking users embedded in what researchers call WEIRD societies: Western, Educated, Industrialized, Rich, and Democratic. The result is not merely a linguistic skew but an epistemological one. Models trained on this data absorb particular assumptions about what counts as knowledge, how arguments should be structured, what historical events matter, and which moral frameworks are treated as universal rather than culturally situated.

This matters acutely in education, where the stakes of cultural representation are high. When an AI tutoring system explains a concept, recommends a narrative frame, or evaluates a student's reasoning, it is not operating from a neutral vantage point. It is drawing on a worldview. For students in sub-Saharan Africa, South and Southeast Asia, Indigenous communities, or the Arabic-speaking world, the implicit assumptions baked into these systems can render their own knowledge traditions invisible or, worse, position them as deviations from a norm. Oral knowledge systems, non-linear conceptions of time, communal rather than individualist epistemologies — these are not well represented in the training corpora of GPT-class models, and their absence is not incidental.

The problem operates at multiple levels. At the surface level, there is the straightforward matter of language coverage: models perform markedly worse in Swahili, Bengali, or Quechua than in English or French, producing outputs that are less fluent, less accurate, and more prone to error. But beneath this lies a deeper issue of conceptual translation. Even when models are fine-tuned or prompted to operate in other languages, they often carry forward Western conceptual scaffolding, effectively translating not just words but assumptions. A model asked to discuss family structure, civic responsibility, or the nature of historical progress in Yoruba may produce grammatically correct Yoruba while still reasoning from implicitly Anglo-American premises.

Several technical and institutional responses have been proposed, each with genuine promise and genuine limitations. One approach involves diversifying training data — deliberately incorporating multilingual corpora, non-Western academic traditions, and community-generated content from underrepresented regions. Projects such as Masakhane, a grassroots initiative focused on African natural language processing, represent serious efforts in this direction, building datasets and benchmarks that reflect African languages and contexts. The evidence suggests such targeted data enrichment can meaningfully improve model performance and cultural fidelity for specific communities, though the scale of effort required is substantial and the coverage remains uneven.

A second approach involves fine-tuning or retrieval-augmented generation, where a general-purpose model is adapted using locally curated content — regional curricula, indigenous knowledge repositories, community-validated texts. This is more tractable than retraining from scratch and has shown practical results in pilot educational deployments. The limitation is that it places the burden of cultural correction on local communities and institutions that may lack the technical infrastructure or resources to undertake it systematically.

A third, more contested approach involves the alignment and reinforcement learning processes that shape model behaviour after initial training. If human feedback used to align models is itself drawn from culturally homogeneous annotator pools — as it frequently is — then alignment may entrench rather than correct existing biases. Some researchers argue for diversifying annotator demographics and explicitly incorporating cross-cultural value frameworks into alignment protocols. Others caution that this risks introducing new forms of essentialism, treating cultures as monolithic when they are internally diverse and contested.

There is also a structural argument that no purely technical fix is sufficient. The governance of AI development — who funds it, who sets research agendas, who sits on ethics boards — remains concentrated in a small number of wealthy, predominantly Western institutions and corporations. Without meaningful redistribution of that power, technical interventions risk being cosmetic. This view, associated with scholars working in the tradition of postcolonial science and technology studies, holds that bias in AI is not an engineering problem with an engineering solution but a political problem requiring political remedies: international governance frameworks, community data sovereignty, and genuine co-development rather than adaptation of tools designed elsewhere.

What is established is that the bias is real, measurable, and consequential for educational equity. What remains genuinely open is the question of remedy. The most honest position is that addressing cultural and ideological bias in educational AI requires simultaneous action at multiple levels — technical, institutional, and political — and that progress on any single front, without the others, is likely to be insufficient. For educators and policymakers deploying these tools, the immediate practical obligation is transparency: being explicit with students about what these systems are, where they come from, and what they do not know.

What ethical obligations do AI companies have toward education systems they disrupt but do not serve?

## Disruption Without Obligation: The Ethical Debt AI Companies Owe to Education

When a technology reshapes the conditions under which learning occurs, the institutions most affected are rarely the ones who chose to adopt it. General-purpose AI systems — large language models capable of writing essays, solving problem sets, and tutoring students — have altered the landscape of education not because schools invited them in, but because students and the broader culture did. This asymmetry sits at the heart of one of the more pressing ethical questions in contemporary technology policy: what obligations, if any, do AI companies bear toward education systems they disrupt but do not formally serve?

The most documented dimension of this problem is what analysts at the AI Now Institute described in 2023 as the externalization of disruption costs. AI companies capture the economic value generated by their products while the burdens of adaptation fall elsewhere — onto teachers redesigning assessments, onto administrators drafting integrity policies, onto curriculum developers rethinking what skills still matter when a machine can perform them on demand. Public education systems, already operating under fiscal constraint, absorb these costs without compensation or consultation. The structural gap between the pace of AI deployment and the accountability mechanisms governing its educational impact is not incidental; it reflects a broader pattern in which corporate responsibility frameworks, designed largely around consumer protection and data privacy, have no adequate category for diffuse institutional harm.

This framing draws on a well-established tradition in political economy: the concept of negative externalities, where private actors impose costs on third parties who have no contractual relationship with them and therefore no obvious legal recourse. What makes the educational case distinctive is the nature of the institution being disrupted. Schools are not merely economic actors who can adjust prices or exit markets. They are public goods entrusted with the formation of future citizens, and their integrity — epistemic, civic, and developmental — is not easily monetized or restored once compromised. When AI makes academic dishonesty trivially easy, or when it accelerates credential inflation by rendering certain demonstrated competencies obsolete, the damage is real but diffuse, making it resistant to the tort-based remedies that might apply in other contexts.

The question of what obligations follow from this disruption is genuinely contested, and the debate divides along both empirical and normative lines. One position holds that AI companies bear a duty of proactive engagement: they should fund research into educational impact, share data with institutions studying that impact, and participate in the co-design of policies governing their tools' use in academic settings. This view treats disruption as generating a kind of ethical debt, analogous to the obligations that pharmaceutical companies bear toward healthcare systems or that extractive industries bear toward affected communities. The analogy is imperfect — AI companies are not directly polluting schools — but the underlying logic is similar: those who profit from a technology that imposes systemic costs on public institutions have some obligation to help manage those costs.

A competing position is more skeptical of voluntary obligation frameworks and argues instead for regulatory intervention. On this view, asking AI companies to self-impose educational responsibilities is naive; the incentive structures of competitive technology markets push relentlessly toward deployment speed, and ethical commitments made without enforcement mechanisms tend to dissolve under commercial pressure. What is needed, this argument holds, is mandatory impact assessment before general-purpose AI systems are released, along with ongoing reporting requirements that give education policymakers the information they need to respond. The European Union's AI Act gestures in this direction by classifying certain educational applications as high-risk, though critics note that its provisions address direct deployment rather than the indirect disruption caused by tools not marketed to schools at all.

There is also a more fundamental tension lurking beneath these policy debates, one that concerns the nature of educational value itself. If AI can perform many of the tasks that schooling has historically trained students to perform, the disruption is not merely administrative but philosophical. It raises questions about what education is for — whether its purpose is the transmission of skills that the labor market rewards, or the cultivation of capacities that resist automation: judgment, ethical reasoning, the ability to situate knowledge within human experience. AI companies have no particular expertise in answering these questions, and there is a reasonable argument that they should not be the ones to answer them. But their products are already shaping the answers by default, which is itself a form of influence that carries ethical weight.

What seems clear, even amid these contested questions, is that the current arrangement — in which AI companies deploy transformative tools, education systems scramble to respond, and no formal mechanism connects the two — is ethically inadequate. The minimum obligation is transparency: honest, detailed disclosure of how these systems work, what they can and cannot do, and what evidence exists about their effects on learning. Beyond that minimum, the shape of obligation remains genuinely open, which is itself a reason for urgency rather than complacency.

Chapter 3. Chapter 3: AI's Role in Teaching

What can AI tutors do better than human teachers, and where do they categorically fail?

## AI Tutors and Human Teachers: Complementary Strengths, Categorical Limits

The question of what artificial intelligence can do better than a human teacher — and where it fundamentally cannot compete — is one of the most consequential in contemporary education. Decades of research, beginning with work on Intelligent Tutoring Systems long before the era of large language models, offer a surprisingly clear picture, though important tensions and open questions remain.

The strongest empirical case for AI tutoring rests on what researchers call the "2-sigma problem," identified by Benjamin Bloom in 1984. Bloom demonstrated that students receiving one-on-one human tutoring outperformed classroom-taught peers by approximately two standard deviations — a staggering advantage that conventional schooling, constrained by teacher-to-student ratios, could rarely provide. The central promise of AI tutoring has always been to democratise that advantage. VanLehn's landmark 2011 meta-analysis of fourteen Intelligent Tutoring Systems found effect sizes of roughly 0.76 compared to classroom instruction — substantial, if not quite reaching Bloom's two-sigma ceiling. This is an established finding, replicated across multiple systems and subject domains, and it anchors the legitimate optimism surrounding AI tutors.

The mechanism behind these gains is reasonably well understood. AI tutors excel at personalised pacing: they can identify precisely where a student's understanding breaks down, adjust the difficulty of subsequent problems in real time, and provide immediate, targeted feedback without the delays inherent in a classroom where one teacher must attend to thirty students simultaneously. A human teacher, however skilled, cannot monitor every student's cognitive state moment to moment. An AI system, by contrast, does nothing else. This continuous, granular responsiveness is not merely a convenience — it reflects a genuine structural advantage. Errors are caught before they calcify into misconceptions; mastery is confirmed before new material is introduced. In procedural domains such as mathematics, introductory programming, and grammar, where correctness is unambiguous and practice volume matters, this advantage is well-documented and meaningful.

AI tutors also offer consistency and availability that no human teacher can match. They do not have bad days, do not unconsciously favour students who remind them of themselves, and are accessible at two in the morning before an examination. For students in under-resourced schools or remote areas, an AI tutor may represent the only form of individualised academic support available. This equity argument is not merely rhetorical; it reflects a real gap that AI systems are structurally positioned to address.

Yet the catalogue of failures is equally instructive, and in several respects categorical rather than merely technical. The most fundamental concerns the nature of understanding itself. Current AI tutors — even those powered by sophisticated language models — operate on pattern recognition and probabilistic text generation. They can identify that a student has made an error and suggest a correction, but they do not comprehend the student's reasoning in the way a perceptive human teacher does. A skilled teacher notices the particular confusion behind a wrong answer, distinguishes a careless slip from a deep misconception, and responds to the student's thinking rather than merely to its surface expression. This interpretive depth remains beyond what AI systems reliably achieve.

Equally important is the relational dimension of teaching, which research consistently identifies as central to motivation, persistence, and long-term engagement. Students learn more from teachers they trust, feel seen by, and believe care about them. This is not sentiment — it is a robust finding in educational psychology. AI systems can simulate warmth, but the simulation is recognised as such by most students beyond early childhood, and the affective bond that sustains a struggling learner through difficulty is not something a chatbot can authentically provide. Related to this is the AI tutor's near-total incapacity for the kind of moral and civic education that happens in classrooms: the negotiation of disagreement, the modelling of intellectual humility, the cultivation of collaborative reasoning among peers. These are not peripheral to education; they are among its central purposes.

There is also a contested but important concern about dependency and the atrophying of productive struggle. Some researchers argue that the immediacy of AI feedback, while beneficial for skill acquisition, may reduce students' tolerance for the uncertainty and effort that genuine learning requires. If a system intervenes too quickly, it may prevent the desirable difficulty that consolidates long-term retention. This remains an open empirical question, but it points to a design tension that has not been resolved.

The honest synthesis, then, is that AI tutors are genuinely superior to human teachers in a narrow but important set of conditions: high-volume procedural practice, immediate corrective feedback, adaptive pacing, and consistent availability at scale. They fail categorically — not merely in degree but in kind — when education requires authentic relationship, interpretive understanding of a student's inner life, moral modelling, or the cultivation of collaborative human judgment. The error would be to treat these as competing alternatives. The evidence suggests they are complementary, and that the most productive question is not which is better, but how each can be deployed where its advantages are real.

How should the teacher's role be redefined — from transmitter of knowledge to what, exactly?

## The Teacher Transformed: Redefining the Educator's Role in an Age of Intelligent Machines

The question of what teachers are for has always lurked beneath the surface of educational debate, but artificial intelligence has forced it into the open with unusual urgency. When a well-prompted language model can explain the causes of the First World War, walk a student through quadratic equations, or provide instant feedback on a draft essay, the traditional justification for the teacher as primary conduit of knowledge becomes difficult to sustain. What remains — and what, arguably, becomes more valuable than ever — is everything that content delivery was never really about in the first place.

The most compelling reframing to emerge from contemporary educational research positions the teacher as a learning architect: someone whose primary work is designing the environments, sequences, challenges, and relationships within which genuine understanding can develop. This perspective, associated prominently with David Perkins and the tradition of Harvard's Project Zero, rests on a straightforward premise. If AI systems can deliver content more efficiently, accurately, and adaptively than any human lecturer, then the teacher's comparative advantage lies elsewhere — in the craft of constructing experiences that provoke curiosity, sustain effort, and cultivate the habits of mind that no algorithm yet reliably instils.

The architectural metaphor is instructive precisely because architecture is not mere decoration. A learning architect makes consequential decisions: which problems are worth wrestling with, in what order, with what degree of productive struggle, and toward what larger purposes. These are fundamentally human judgements, requiring knowledge of particular students in particular contexts — their prior experiences, their anxieties, their emerging identities as learners. An AI system optimising for measurable performance metrics cannot easily replicate the teacher's capacity to notice that a student's disengagement signals something worth attending to, or that a classroom's energy is ripe for a digression that will prove more educationally significant than the planned lesson.

This leads to a second, closely related redefinition: the teacher as mentor and meaning-maker. Research on motivation and self-determination consistently shows that students learn more deeply when they experience their work as meaningful and when they feel genuinely known by a trusted adult. The relational dimension of teaching — the capacity to model intellectual curiosity, to demonstrate that difficulty is survivable, to connect academic content to a student's emerging sense of self — is not incidental to learning but constitutive of it. AI tutors can be patient and responsive, but the evidence that they can provide the kind of mentorship that shapes a young person's relationship to knowledge over years remains, at present, thin.

A third strand of the emerging consensus concerns the teacher as critical interlocutor — someone who helps students navigate, evaluate, and interrogate the information that AI systems produce. This role is genuinely new. Previous generations of teachers could reasonably assume that the information environment, however imperfect, was not actively generating plausible-sounding falsehoods at scale. The proliferation of AI-generated content changes that assumption. Teachers who can model epistemic humility, demonstrate how to interrogate sources, and create classroom cultures in which questioning is valued over mere answer-retrieval become essential precisely because AI makes uncritical consumption so frictionless.

It would be intellectually dishonest, however, to present this reframing as settled or uncontested. Several tensions remain genuinely open. One concerns equity: the vision of the teacher as learning architect presupposes educators with the time, training, and institutional support to design rich experiences rather than simply manage crowded classrooms under resource constraints. In many school systems globally, those conditions do not exist, and there is a real risk that the "new teacher role" becomes another aspiration that lands most heavily on those already least supported. A second tension concerns the knowledge base itself. Some educational researchers argue that the transmission model is being abandoned too hastily — that teachers who deeply understand their subject matter remain irreplaceable not because AI cannot explain content, but because expert human knowledge enables the kind of improvisational responsiveness to student thinking that no pre-designed system can match. On this view, the learning architect and the subject-matter expert are not alternatives but complements, and the danger lies in training teachers to facilitate experiences they do not themselves deeply understand.

What the evidence does support, with reasonable confidence, is that the teacher's role cannot remain unchanged and should not be defended in its traditional form simply out of institutional inertia. The transmission of information was always the least interesting thing teachers did. What AI makes newly visible is the irreducible human core of the work: the design of meaningful challenge, the cultivation of relationship, the modelling of intellectual life, and the guidance of students through an information environment that increasingly requires critical navigation. Redefining the teacher's role is not a concession to technology but a clarification of what good teaching always was.

What pedagogical models (Socratic, constructivist, mastery-based) does AI enable at scale for the first time?

# Pedagogical Models AI Enables at Scale

For most of the history of formal education, the most powerful ways of teaching have also been the least scalable. The Socratic dialogue, the constructivist project, the mastery-based progression — each demands something that mass instruction cannot provide: sustained, responsive attention to an individual learner's current state of understanding. A single teacher facing thirty students must compress, standardize, and approximate. What artificial intelligence introduces, at least in principle, is the possibility of dissolving that trade-off — of delivering pedagogically sophisticated instruction to thousands of learners simultaneously without collapsing into the one-size-fits-all logic that has long defined institutional schooling.

The Socratic method is perhaps the most demanding of the three models to replicate, because it is fundamentally a conversational art. It requires an interlocutor who can detect the precise shape of a student's misconception, formulate a question that exposes rather than corrects it, and then follow the student's response into whatever territory it opens. Historically, this has meant small seminars or one-on-one tutoring — contexts available to a privileged minority of learners. Intelligent Tutoring Systems have been working toward a computational approximation of this dynamic for decades. AutoTutor, developed by Arthur Graesser and colleagues, uses natural language processing to engage students in dialogue about physics, computer literacy, and critical thinking, posing questions, detecting gaps in reasoning, and nudging students toward self-correction rather than simply supplying answers. The evidence base for such systems is now substantial: meta-analyses of ITS research consistently show effect sizes in the range of 0.4 to 0.8 standard deviations compared to conventional classroom instruction — a meaningful gain, though researchers are careful to note that these comparisons depend heavily on what counts as the control condition. The arrival of large language models has extended these capabilities considerably, enabling more fluent and contextually sensitive dialogue than earlier rule-based systems could manage, though whether LLM-based tutors genuinely replicate Socratic reasoning or merely simulate its surface features remains an open and important question.

Constructivism presents a different kind of scaling challenge. Where Socratic tutoring is dialogic, constructivist pedagogy is generative: it asks students to build knowledge through exploration, experimentation, and the construction of artifacts — essays, models, designs, solutions to open-ended problems. The difficulty of scaling this approach has always been evaluative. Open-ended work resists automated assessment, and without timely, substantive feedback, the constructivist loop breaks down. AI changes this in several ways. Generative AI systems can now engage with student-produced work at a level of specificity that was previously impossible to automate — commenting not just on surface features but on the coherence of an argument, the validity of a model, or the originality of a design approach. Platforms built around project-based learning are beginning to use AI to scaffold the construction process itself, prompting students with questions at moments of apparent stagnation and offering resources calibrated to the direction a student's inquiry is taking. This remains an emerging area, and the research base is thinner than for ITS; there are legitimate concerns about whether AI feedback on creative or argumentative work is reliable enough to support genuine constructivist learning, or whether it introduces systematic biases that distort the process.

Mastery-based learning — the model associated with Benjamin Bloom's famous "2 sigma problem" — may be the area where AI's scaling potential is most clearly established. Bloom demonstrated in 1984 that one-on-one tutoring, which allows a student to advance only upon demonstrating genuine mastery of each concept, produced learning outcomes two standard deviations above conventional group instruction. The obstacle was always logistical: individualized mastery progression requires continuous assessment and curriculum branching that no human teacher can sustain across a full classroom. Adaptive learning systems have been addressing this problem for years, and the evidence that well-designed adaptive platforms improve learning outcomes — particularly in mathematics and the sciences — is among the more robust findings in educational technology research. Systems like those deployed by Khan Academy and various university-level adaptive courseware providers track student performance at a granular level, identify prerequisite gaps, and route learners through content sequences tailored to their demonstrated needs. The integration of more sophisticated AI has made these systems more responsive and more capable of distinguishing between a student who has memorized a procedure and one who has understood the underlying principle.

What unites these three models is that each, in its classical form, required a human expert to hold a detailed, dynamic model of an individual learner's mind. That requirement was the bottleneck. AI does not eliminate the need for such a model — it attempts to construct and maintain one computationally, updating it in real time as the learner responds. Whether the computational model is ever rich enough to fully substitute for a skilled human teacher is genuinely contested. What is less contested is that it can approximate these pedagogical ideals at a scale, and at a cost, that makes them accessible to learners who would otherwise encounter only their pale institutional substitutes.

How can AI support teachers (planning, assessment, feedback) without deskilling or replacing them?

## The Teacher and the Machine: How AI Can Assist Without Diminishing

The question of whether artificial intelligence can genuinely support teachers — rather than gradually hollowing out their professional role — sits at the centre of one of education's most consequential contemporary debates. The evidence available suggests that the answer is a qualified yes, but one that depends heavily on how AI tools are designed, deployed, and understood by the educators who use them.

The most concrete and well-documented benefit concerns the sheer weight of preparatory labour that teaching demands. According to a 2023 RAND Corporation survey of American teachers, educators who incorporated AI tools into their lesson planning reported saving between one and three hours per week on administrative and preparatory tasks. That is not a trivial gain. Teacher burnout and attrition are persistent crises in many school systems, and time reclaimed from routine preparation is time that can be redirected toward the relational, responsive, and intellectually demanding work that defines good teaching. Tools such as ChatGPT and purpose-built platforms like MagicSchool have demonstrated a genuine capacity to generate draft lesson structures, suggest differentiated activities, and compile resource lists at speed no human could match.

Yet the same RAND research introduces an important qualification: the pedagogical appropriateness of AI-generated plans varied significantly without teacher intervention. This finding is crucial, and it reframes the entire question. AI, in its current form, produces outputs that are plausible and often serviceable, but not reliably attuned to the specific students in a specific classroom, the local curriculum context, the unspoken dynamics of a particular school community, or the subtle professional judgements that experienced teachers carry as tacit knowledge. A lesson plan that looks coherent on screen may be pedagogically misaligned in practice. The implication is not that AI planning tools are without value, but that they function best as a starting point — a draft to be interrogated, revised, and owned by the teacher — rather than as a finished product to be adopted wholesale.

This distinction between AI as co-pilot and AI as autopilot is more than metaphorical. When teachers engage critically with AI-generated material, selecting what fits, discarding what does not, and adapting the rest, they are exercising precisely the professional judgement that defines their expertise. The risk of deskilling arises not from using AI but from using it passively — accepting outputs without scrutiny, gradually outsourcing the deliberative process that keeps pedagogical skill sharp. The technology itself is largely neutral on this point; the danger lies in institutional cultures or workload pressures that incentivise speed over reflection.

In the domains of assessment and feedback, the picture is similarly nuanced. AI systems capable of analysing student writing, flagging patterns of misunderstanding, or generating formative feedback at scale offer genuine promise, particularly in large classes where individual attention is structurally constrained. Automated feedback can be immediate in a way that human feedback rarely is, and immediacy matters for learning. However, the quality of AI-generated feedback on complex or creative work remains contested. Systems trained on large corpora can identify surface-level errors with reasonable accuracy, but they struggle with the interpretive, contextual, and motivational dimensions of feedback — knowing when a student needs encouragement rather than correction, or when an unconventional response reflects genuine insight rather than confusion. These are judgements that require knowing the student, and AI does not know students in any meaningful sense.

There is also a structural concern worth naming honestly. If AI takes over the feedback loop between teacher and student, even partially, it risks attenuating one of the most educationally significant relationships in a school. Research on effective teaching consistently identifies the teacher-student relationship as a primary driver of engagement and learning. Feedback is not merely information transfer; it is a form of attention, and students know the difference between a response generated by a system and one written by a person who has read their work carefully. The pedagogical value of human feedback is partly constituted by its human origin.

The most defensible model, then, is one in which AI handles the high-volume, lower-stakes, and more mechanical dimensions of teacher work — generating initial drafts, flagging patterns in assessment data, producing differentiated resource options — while teachers retain authority over the interpretive, relational, and evaluative dimensions that require professional judgement. This is not a comfortable equilibrium that will maintain itself automatically. It requires deliberate institutional design, professional development that builds AI literacy alongside critical scepticism, and a clear-eyed commitment to preserving the conditions under which teaching as a skilled profession can flourish.

The technology, in short, is neither saviour nor threat by nature. What determines its effect on the teaching profession is the wisdom — or its absence — with which it is integrated into the work.

What does effective human–AI co-teaching look like in a real classroom?

## Human–AI Co-Teaching in the Real Classroom

The question of what effective human–AI co-teaching actually looks like in practice—not in theory, not in pilot demonstrations, but in ordinary classrooms with ordinary constraints—has begun to yield concrete answers, though the picture remains uneven and contested in important respects. The most instructive evidence comes from large-scale deployments of adaptive intelligent tutoring systems, particularly Carnegie Learning's MATHia platform in U.S. middle and high schools, which represents one of the most empirically documented cases of this emerging pedagogical arrangement.

The structural logic of the MATHia model is worth examining carefully, because it reveals a division of cognitive and relational labour that appears, under the right conditions, to be genuinely productive. The AI system takes responsibility for what it does well: individualising practice sequences, diagnosing errors in real time, adjusting problem difficulty in response to student performance, and maintaining a granular record of each learner's progress. The human teacher, meanwhile, is freed from the exhausting and often impossible task of simultaneously tracking thirty students' individual trajectories. Instead, the teacher consults a "class snapshot" dashboard that surfaces which students are struggling, where misconceptions cluster, and which learners may be coasting without challenge. Armed with this intelligence, the teacher circulates and intervenes—not randomly, not according to who raises a hand, but with a degree of targeting that unaided human attention cannot sustain across a full classroom.

This is not a trivial shift. One of the persistent structural problems in mixed-ability classrooms is that teacher attention is distributed inequitably, often gravitating toward students who are most vocal or most visibly distressed, while quieter strugglers go unnoticed. An AI system that continuously monitors all students simultaneously and surfaces need in a legible format addresses this problem directly. The teacher's role becomes less that of a universal explainer and more that of a diagnostician and relationship-builder—someone who can offer the kind of contextualised, emotionally attuned support that no current AI system can replicate.

What the evidence from MATHia deployments suggests, however, is that this model's effectiveness is highly contingent on teacher behaviour. When teachers treat the AI dashboard as a passive report rather than an active prompt for intervention—when they allow students to work through the system without circulating, questioning, or supplementing—the benefits diminish substantially. The AI, in other words, does not replace pedagogical judgement; it depends on it. Effective co-teaching requires teachers who understand what the system's metrics mean, who can translate a flagged misconception into a productive conversation, and who know when to override the system's sequencing in favour of whole-class discussion or collaborative problem-solving.

This points to a broader principle that emerges from the available evidence: the human and the AI are not interchangeable partners of equal standing, nor is the AI simply a tool the teacher operates. The relationship is better understood as a genuine division of complementary functions, where each party's contribution is necessary but insufficient on its own. The AI provides scale, consistency, and data; the teacher provides interpretation, relationship, and professional judgement. When either side of this partnership is weak—when the AI's adaptive model is poorly calibrated, or when the teacher lacks the training to act on its outputs—the arrangement underperforms.

There are legitimate tensions in this picture that the evidence does not fully resolve. One concerns equity: schools with stronger teacher professional development, more stable technology infrastructure, and smaller class sizes are better positioned to realise the model's potential. In under-resourced settings, the same system can function as a substitute for teacher attention rather than a complement to it, with students working through adaptive exercises in relative isolation. Whether this represents a net gain over the status quo is genuinely debatable, and researchers have not reached consensus.

A second tension concerns the scope of what AI co-teaching can address. The MATHia evidence is drawn almost entirely from mathematics, a domain where correctness is relatively unambiguous and where adaptive sequencing of procedural and conceptual problems is tractable. Whether analogous models can work in writing instruction, historical reasoning, or scientific argumentation—domains where the criteria for good performance are more complex and contested—remains an open and important question. Early experiments in AI-assisted writing feedback suggest some promise, but the evidence base is far thinner and the risks of reductive feedback loops are more pronounced.

What the best current evidence supports, then, is a cautiously optimistic but conditional picture. Effective human–AI co-teaching is not a technology problem that good software solves automatically; it is a pedagogical and organisational achievement that requires deliberate role design, teacher preparation, and ongoing attention to whether the partnership is serving students or merely processing them. The classroom in which this works well is one where the AI handles the relentless cognitive bookkeeping of individualised learning, and the teacher—informed, attentive, and professionally confident—does what only a human can.

How should teacher training change so educators can critically use, evaluate, and challenge AI tools?

## Preparing Educators for the Age of Intelligent Machines

The arrival of sophisticated AI tools in classrooms has exposed a striking gap between the pace of technological change and the readiness of the teaching profession to meet it. According to a 2023 UNESCO report, fewer than 10% of teacher training institutions globally had developed formal frameworks for preparing educators to work with AI tools, and where AI appeared in curricula at all, it was typically framed as a productivity aid rather than as a subject requiring critical scrutiny. This is not a minor curricular oversight. It represents a structural failure to equip the people most responsible for shaping young minds with the conceptual tools they need to evaluate, challenge, and responsibly deploy technologies that are already reshaping learning environments.

The dominant approach to AI in professional development has been instrumental: teachers are shown how to use a tool, not how to interrogate it. This framing treats AI systems as essentially neutral, their outputs as reliable, and their adoption as straightforwardly beneficial. Yet the research literature makes clear that AI tools used in education carry embedded assumptions about what counts as good writing, correct reasoning, or appropriate behaviour — assumptions that reflect the data on which these systems were trained and the choices made by their designers. A teacher who cannot identify these assumptions cannot protect students from their distortions, let alone help students develop the critical awareness to do so themselves.

What, then, should change? The evidence points in several interconnected directions. First, teacher education must move beyond tool familiarity toward what researchers have begun calling AI literacy in its fuller sense: an understanding of how machine learning systems work at a conceptual level, what kinds of errors they characteristically make, how their outputs can be evaluated, and what institutional and commercial interests shape their design. This does not require training teachers as computer scientists. It does require that educators understand, for instance, why a language model might produce fluent but factually wrong text, why an automated grading system might disadvantage students from certain linguistic backgrounds, or why a recommendation algorithm might narrow rather than broaden a student's intellectual exposure.

Second, and perhaps more fundamentally, teacher training must cultivate a disposition of principled scepticism toward AI outputs — not hostility, but the same critical stance a well-trained reader brings to any source. This is harder to teach than technical knowledge because it runs against the grain of how AI tools are typically marketed, which emphasises confidence, efficiency, and comprehensiveness. Programs that embed AI evaluation into existing subject-specific pedagogy — asking student teachers to compare an AI-generated history essay with a student-written one, or to audit an AI reading-level assessment against their own professional judgment — appear more effective at building this disposition than standalone AI modules, though the evidence base here remains relatively thin and context-dependent.

Third, the ethical dimensions of AI use must be treated as substantive professional knowledge rather than as a compliance checklist. Questions about data privacy, algorithmic bias, the erosion of student agency, and the appropriate boundaries of automated assessment are not peripheral concerns to be addressed in a single seminar. They are central to what it means to teach responsibly in the current moment. Programs that integrate these questions across multiple courses and connect them to real cases from classroom practice produce more durable changes in teacher thinking than those that treat ethics as a discrete module.

There is genuine tension in the research about how prescriptive training should be. Some scholars argue for clear institutional frameworks that specify which AI tools are appropriate for which purposes, giving teachers structured guidance. Others contend that overly prescriptive approaches risk producing compliance rather than genuine critical engagement, and that teachers need the intellectual freedom to develop their own evaluative frameworks through practice and reflection. Both positions have merit, and the tension between them is unlikely to be resolved by evidence alone — it reflects deeper disagreements about the nature of professional autonomy in teaching.

What is not contested is the urgency. AI tools are already in classrooms, often introduced by students before institutions have formed any policy response. Teachers who have not been prepared to evaluate these tools are not neutral bystanders; they are, by default, passive endorsers of whatever the technology does. The profession cannot afford to treat AI literacy as an optional enhancement to teacher education. It belongs at the core of what it means to be a prepared, responsible educator — alongside subject knowledge, pedagogical skill, and the capacity to understand and respond to the particular human beings in one's care. The institutions responsible for teacher preparation have been slow to recognise this, and the cost of that slowness is being paid in classrooms every day.

Can AI meaningfully teach non-cognitive skills — empathy, collaboration, resilience, character?

## Can AI Meaningfully Teach Non-Cognitive Skills — Empathy, Collaboration, Resilience, Character?

The question of whether artificial intelligence can teach empathy, collaboration, resilience, and character cuts to something fundamental about what education is actually for. These capacities are not merely supplementary to academic achievement; developmental psychologists and employers alike regard them as central to human flourishing and professional effectiveness. Yet they are also precisely the qualities that seem most resistant to algorithmic instruction — rooted as they are in lived experience, moral formation, and the irreducibly social texture of human relationships. The evidence accumulated so far suggests a picture that is neither dismissive nor triumphalist: AI can demonstrably improve certain component skills associated with non-cognitive development, but whether this constitutes genuinely teaching empathy or character in any deep sense remains an open and philosophically serious question.

The most concrete evidence comes from AI-powered social-emotional learning platforms. Tools such as Kognito's avatar-based simulations and Mursion's virtual reality role-play environments have been studied in controlled settings, and the results are measurable. Participants in these programmes show statistically significant improvements in discrete, observable behaviours: perspective-taking exercises, active listening techniques, and conflict de-escalation strategies. These are not trivial gains. The ability to pause before responding in a tense conversation, to articulate another person's position before rebutting it, or to recognise emotional cues in a difficult exchange — these are learnable micro-skills, and AI-driven simulation appears capable of drilling them effectively. The safe, repeatable, low-stakes environment that AI provides is genuinely valuable here: a trainee teacher or medical student can rehearse a difficult conversation dozens of times without exhausting a real human interlocutor or risking harm to a real patient.

The caveats, however, are substantial. The gains documented in these studies tend to cluster around behaviours that are measurable precisely because they are somewhat mechanical — the outward form of empathy rather than its inner substance. Whether a learner who has practised perspective-taking with a virtual avatar has thereby become more empathetic as a person, or has simply become more skilled at performing empathetic behaviour, is a distinction the current evidence cannot resolve. This is not a pedantic philosophical quibble. Character, in the classical sense that educators from Aristotle to contemporary virtue ethicists have described, is not a set of techniques but a stable disposition — a reliable tendency to act well across varied and unpredictable circumstances, including those where no one is watching and no simulation has prepared you. AI can rehearse the script; it cannot yet guarantee the internalization.

Resilience presents a related puzzle. Some adaptive learning platforms attempt to build resilience by engineering productive struggle — calibrating difficulty so that students encounter setbacks they can overcome with effort. There is reasonable evidence that this kind of scaffolded challenge can improve persistence on academic tasks. But resilience in the fuller sense — the capacity to recover from genuine loss, failure, or grief — is formed through real adversity and, crucially, through relationships with people who have themselves navigated difficulty. An algorithm that adjusts a maths problem's difficulty level is doing something categorically different from a mentor who has failed and recovered and can speak to that experience with authority and vulnerability.

Collaboration is perhaps the domain where AI's limitations are most structural. Genuine collaboration requires negotiating with people whose interests, communication styles, and emotional states are genuinely unpredictable — and who matter to you in ways that a virtual agent does not. AI can simulate collaborative scenarios, but the simulation is always, at some level, a controlled environment. The friction, the compromise, the occasional rupture and repair that characterise real teamwork are difficult to replicate when one party in the interaction is a programme designed to be pedagogically useful.

Where the evidence points most clearly is toward a complementary rather than substitutive role. AI tools appear most effective when they prepare learners for human interaction rather than replacing it — when a student practises a difficult conversation with an avatar before having it with a real peer, or when a teacher uses AI-generated data about a student's emotional patterns to inform a human mentoring conversation. The technology, in this framing, is a rehearsal space and a diagnostic instrument, not the primary site of character formation.

What remains genuinely contested is the long-term transfer question: do skills practised in AI-mediated environments persist and generalise into real-world behaviour? The existing studies are largely short-term and context-specific. Longitudinal evidence is sparse. Until that gap is filled, the most intellectually honest position is that AI has demonstrated real utility in developing the behavioural components of non-cognitive skills, while the deeper work of forming empathetic, resilient, collaborative human beings remains — as it has always been — a fundamentally human undertaking.

How do we assess learning authentically when AI can complete any traditional assessment?

## Assessing Learning Authentically When AI Can Complete Any Traditional Assessment

The emergence of large language models capable of producing polished essays, solving complex mathematical problems, and answering examination questions with apparent fluency has precipitated what assessment scholars are calling a "validity crisis" in higher education. Research from the University of Sydney's Centre for Educational Measurement and Assessment has identified the core problem with precision: standard written assignments, multiple-choice tests, and take-home examinations can no longer reliably distinguish between a student's own understanding and the output of a generative AI system. When an instrument cannot measure what it claims to measure, its validity collapses — and with it, the evidentiary basis for grades, credentials, and progression decisions.

The instinct to respond by detecting AI use has proven largely futile. Detection tools operate on probabilistic inference rather than certainty, producing both false positives that wrongly accuse students and false negatives that miss genuine misuse. More fundamentally, the detection arms race misframes the problem: the question is not how to catch students using AI, but how to design assessments that reveal genuine learning regardless of whether AI is available. This reframing, increasingly endorsed across the assessment literature, shifts attention from policing to design.

The most robust response emerging from research is a return to — and reinvention of — authentic assessment: tasks that require students to demonstrate understanding in ways that are situated, personal, and resistant to wholesale delegation. Oral examinations, long dismissed in many disciplines as impractical at scale, have attracted renewed scholarly interest precisely because they demand real-time reasoning that cannot be outsourced. When a student must explain their thinking, respond to unexpected follow-up questions, and defend a position under gentle interrogation, the examiner is observing cognition directly rather than inferring it from a text artifact. The limitation is resource intensity: oral assessment at scale requires significant staff time and introduces its own risks of inconsistency and bias, concerns that remain active in the literature.

Process-based assessment offers a complementary approach. Rather than evaluating only a finished product, educators assess the documented journey — annotated drafts, revision histories, reflective journals, and timestamped working notes. The logic is that AI can produce an endpoint but cannot fabricate a credible intellectual history of a specific student's evolving engagement with a problem. Research into portfolio-based models suggests that when students are asked to narrate their own learning trajectory and connect it to personal experience, the resulting evidence is both harder to simulate and more educationally meaningful. The tension here is that process documentation can itself be gamed by a sufficiently motivated student, and the additional burden on both students and assessors is non-trivial.

A more structurally ambitious response involves redesigning tasks around what might be called "AI-proof specificity" — assessments anchored in local, personal, or ephemeral contexts that a general-purpose AI cannot access. A student asked to analyse a debate that occurred in their own seminar room last Tuesday, or to apply a theoretical framework to an artefact they brought from their own life, is working with material that lies outside any model's training data. Similarly, assessments that require integration of course-specific discussions, instructor feedback, and peer interactions create an evidentiary texture that is genuinely personal. This approach is promising but raises equity concerns: students with richer personal contexts, more confident self-disclosure, or greater access to varied experiences may be advantaged in ways that have nothing to do with disciplinary mastery.

There is also a more radical position gaining traction in some quarters: rather than treating AI as a threat to assessment, redesign assessment to include AI as a tool while evaluating the distinctly human contributions — critical judgment, ethical reasoning, creative direction, and the ability to interrogate and improve AI output. Under this model, the assessed competency shifts from information retrieval and text production toward what might be called "epistemic agency": knowing what to ask, recognising when an answer is wrong or incomplete, and taking responsibility for the quality of a final product. This is arguably closer to the skills graduates will actually need. The objection is that foundational knowledge still matters, and assessments that permit AI use from the outset may allow students to bypass the productive struggle through which deep understanding is built.

What the evidence does not yet support is any single solution. The validity crisis is real, but the field is still in an early, exploratory phase. Oral examinations, process portfolios, contextually specific tasks, and AI-integrated assessments each address part of the problem while introducing new complications. What is clear is that the passive continuation of traditional assessment formats — essays submitted to a portal, unsupervised take-home tests — can no longer be defended as valid measures of individual learning. The challenge now is to build assessment systems that are simultaneously rigorous, equitable, scalable, and genuinely illuminating of what students know and can do.
