Who has legitimate authority to govern AI in education — states, markets, professions, or communities — and on what principles?

# Who Has Legitimate Authority to Govern AI in Education?

The question of who should govern artificial intelligence in education is not merely administrative. It touches on foundational disputes about democratic accountability, the nature of professional expertise, the reach of market logic, and the rights of communities to shape the institutions that form their children. No single actor commands uncontested authority, and the emerging governance landscape reflects a genuine contest among states, markets, professions, and communities — each with plausible claims and real limitations.

The most authoritative normative baseline currently in existence is the UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence, adopted in 2021 by all 193 member states. This instrument is significant not because international recommendations carry binding legal force — they do not — but because it represents the broadest multilateral consensus yet achieved on the principles that should govern AI, including in educational settings. UNESCO positions governments as the primary locus of legitimate authority, calling on states to develop national AI strategies grounded in human rights, human dignity, non-discrimination, and transparency. The logic is straightforward: states are the entities that bear legal obligations under international human rights law, that can compel compliance, and that are — at least in democratic systems — accountable to the populations they govern. Education, moreover, has long been understood as a public good and a state responsibility, which strengthens the case for public authority over the technologies that increasingly mediate it.

Yet state authority is neither uniform nor unproblematic. Governments vary enormously in their capacity to regulate sophisticated AI systems, and in many jurisdictions the technical expertise required to audit algorithmic decision-making simply does not exist within public institutions. There is also a structural tension: states are simultaneously regulators of AI in education and, in many cases, enthusiastic procurers of it. When a ministry of education deploys an AI-driven assessment platform across a national school system, it occupies both sides of the governance relationship at once, which creates obvious accountability gaps. In authoritarian contexts, state authority over educational AI raises sharper concerns still — the same infrastructure that personalises learning can surveil students and enforce ideological conformity.

Markets have, in practice, exercised enormous de facto authority over AI in education, largely because the pace of commercial development has outrun regulatory capacity. EdTech companies have shaped what tools are available, what data are collected, and what pedagogical assumptions are embedded in algorithmic systems, often with minimal public oversight. The market's defenders argue that competition drives innovation and that consumer choice — by schools, districts, or families — constitutes a form of distributed governance. The difficulty is that educational markets are rarely competitive in any robust sense: network effects, data lock-in, and the complexity of evaluating AI systems give incumbent vendors structural advantages, while the costs of poor algorithmic decisions fall on students who had no voice in procurement. Market governance, in short, tends to distribute authority toward those with capital and away from those most affected.

Professional authority offers a different kind of claim. Teachers, school psychologists, curriculum specialists, and educational researchers possess domain knowledge that neither states nor markets reliably hold: they understand child development, learning theory, and the contextual judgements that good teaching requires. There is a growing argument — still more normative than institutionalised — that professional bodies should play a formal role in certifying AI tools for educational use, analogous to the role medical associations play in evaluating clinical technologies. This would ground governance in pedagogical expertise rather than commercial interest or bureaucratic procedure. The limitation is that professional bodies can be slow, conservative, and sometimes captured by incumbent interests of their own; and in many countries, teaching lacks the institutional infrastructure — the licensing boards, the research capacity, the political independence — that would make strong professional governance feasible.

Community authority represents perhaps the most democratically compelling claim, and the least institutionally developed. Parents, students, and local communities have direct stakes in how AI shapes educational experience, and there is a reasonable argument that those most affected by a technology should have meaningful input into its governance. Some jurisdictions have experimented with community consultation requirements for AI procurement, and data rights frameworks increasingly recognise parental and student rights over educational data. But community governance faces real challenges of scale, technical literacy, and representation: whose community, and who speaks for it?

What the evidence suggests is that no single authority is adequate on its own. State governance provides the human-rights framework and the coercive capacity that accountability requires, but needs to be disciplined by genuine democratic participation and supplemented by professional expertise. Markets can drive useful innovation but require robust public regulation to prevent the externalisation of harm onto students. Professions can contribute essential pedagogical judgement but need institutional strengthening. Communities deserve meaningful voice, not merely consultation theatre. The most defensible governance architecture is therefore pluralist and layered — with human rights principles, established through multilateral consensus, setting the non-negotiable floor.

How do developing nations avoid a new "AI colonialism" in education while leapfrogging infrastructure gaps?

## Avoiding AI Colonialism While Leapfrogging Infrastructure: The Challenge for Developing Nations

The arrival of artificial intelligence in global education carries a paradox for the developing world. On one hand, AI-powered tutoring systems, adaptive learning platforms, and automated assessment tools offer genuine possibilities for nations where teacher shortages are acute, classrooms are overcrowded, and educational infrastructure remains thin. On the other hand, scholars and policy analysts have grown increasingly concerned that the wholesale adoption of AI tools designed, owned, and operated in the Global North replicates the structural logic of historical colonialism through new technological means. Navigating between these two realities — seizing the genuine opportunity while resisting extractive dependency — is among the most consequential educational policy challenges of the present decade.

The mechanisms of what researchers have begun calling "AI colonialism" in education are not merely rhetorical. Analysis from institutions including the AI Now Institute identifies at least three interlocking dynamics. The first is data colonialism: when students in Kenya, Bangladesh, or Peru interact with a platform headquartered in Silicon Valley or Shenzhen, the behavioral and learning data they generate flows outward, enriching proprietary models that serve commercial interests elsewhere while leaving local institutions with neither the data nor the analytical capacity to improve their own systems. The second mechanism is epistemic imposition — the embedding of particular cultural assumptions, historical narratives, and pedagogical values into AI curricula that present themselves as universal. A system trained predominantly on English-language content and Western educational frameworks does not become neutral simply because it is deployed in Nairobi or Dhaka; it carries its origins with it. The third mechanism is infrastructural dependency, whereby nations that adopt foreign AI platforms find themselves locked into licensing arrangements, proprietary ecosystems, and technical architectures they cannot modify, audit, or eventually replace without prohibitive cost.

These are structural risks, not hypothetical ones, and they deserve to be taken seriously. Yet the alternative — refusing AI adoption on principle — carries its own costs. The leapfrogging argument has genuine empirical support from adjacent technological history. Mobile banking in sub-Saharan Africa bypassed the branch-banking infrastructure that Western financial systems required, delivering financial inclusion to populations that legacy systems had never reached. There is a plausible analogy in education: AI tutoring systems could, in principle, extend individualized instruction to students in rural areas where qualified teachers are scarce, without waiting for the decades-long process of building conventional educational infrastructure. The question is not whether to engage with AI in education, but on what terms and through what institutional arrangements.

Several strategies have emerged from policy research and early implementation experience, though it must be acknowledged that the evidence base remains thin and much of what follows represents informed prescription rather than established outcome. The most promising direction involves investment in local and regional AI development capacity — supporting universities, government agencies, and civil society organizations in nations of the Global South to build, train, and govern their own educational AI systems. This is expensive and slow, but it is the only approach that addresses dependency at its root. Regional coalitions, such as the African Union's emerging data governance frameworks, offer a model for pooling resources across smaller nations that individually lack the scale to develop sovereign AI infrastructure.

A second strategy concerns data governance. Nations that adopt foreign AI platforms can negotiate, through legislation and procurement conditions, for data residency requirements, open licensing of locally generated training data, and mandatory algorithmic audits. This does not eliminate the power asymmetry between a small ministry of education and a large technology corporation, but it creates legal and contractual leverage that currently goes largely unused. The European Union's approach to data sovereignty, whatever its limitations, demonstrates that regulatory frameworks can shift the terms on which technology companies operate within a jurisdiction.

A third, more contested approach involves prioritizing open-source and interoperable AI tools over proprietary platforms. Open-source systems allow local developers to inspect, modify, and localize the underlying models — translating interfaces, adjusting content, and retraining on locally relevant data. The limitation is that open-source AI still requires significant technical capacity to deploy and maintain, capacity that many developing nations currently lack. This points back to the foundational importance of investing in local technical education and research institutions, which is itself a long-term project.

What is clear from the available evidence is that the window for shaping these dynamics is narrow. AI educational platforms are being adopted rapidly, and the path dependencies created by early adoption are difficult to reverse. Nations and international bodies that treat AI in education as a purely technical or logistical matter — a question of connectivity and device access — while ignoring questions of ownership, data sovereignty, and epistemic authority are likely to find, a generation hence, that they have exchanged one form of educational dependency for another. The leapfrog is possible, but only if the terms of the jump are negotiated with clear eyes.

Chapter 6. Chapter 6: Work, Economy, and Society

Which human capabilities will the labor market actually reward in 10, 20, and 50 years — and how do we teach for an unpredictable future?

## Human Capabilities and the Labor Market: Teaching Across an Uncertain Future

The question of which human capabilities will command economic reward over the coming decades is not merely speculative — it is already being answered, partially and imperfectly, by labor markets responding to the first serious wave of AI-driven automation. The emerging evidence points in a recognizable direction, though the further one looks ahead, the more contested and philosophically fraught the picture becomes.

What economists can say with reasonable confidence about the near term — the next ten years — draws on David Autor's influential task framework, developed at MIT over the past two decades. Autor's research documents a persistent hollowing out of routine cognitive and manual work: the clerical processing, the standardized analysis, the predictable physical tasks that once formed the backbone of middle-skill employment. What has proven resistant to this hollowing, and what now commands a growing wage premium, is a cluster of capabilities loosely grouped under social intelligence, complex communication, and creative problem-solving. These are not merely soft skills in the dismissive sense the phrase sometimes implies. They include the capacity to read unstated social dynamics, to negotiate meaning across contexts of genuine ambiguity, to generate solutions where no algorithm exists to specify the problem correctly in the first place. The evidence that these capabilities are already being rewarded is well-established. The contested question is whether that reward will persist or whether advancing AI systems will erode this frontier as they eroded the last one.

There are serious grounds for both optimism and concern on this point. The optimistic reading holds that social and creative capabilities are grounded in embodied human experience — in the accumulated weight of having lived a life, navigated relationships, and operated within cultural contexts that no training dataset fully captures. On this view, the ten-year horizon looks relatively stable for workers who have developed genuine depth in these areas. The more cautious reading notes that AI systems have already demonstrated surprising competence in tasks once assumed to require human creativity and social sensitivity: generating persuasive prose, producing plausible emotional responses, synthesizing complex information into coherent narratives. Whether these demonstrations represent genuine capability or sophisticated pattern-matching that will fail under pressure remains an open and genuinely important empirical question.

At the twenty-year horizon, the uncertainty compounds in ways that make confident prediction intellectually dishonest. What can be said is that the labor market will almost certainly reward the capacity to work with AI systems rather than in competition with them — what some researchers call "human-AI complementarity." This is not simply a matter of technical literacy, though that matters. It involves the metacognitive ability to evaluate AI outputs critically, to know when a fluent-sounding answer is wrong, and to supply the contextual judgment that automated systems systematically lack. It also involves what might be called epistemic courage: the willingness to maintain a position, take responsibility for a decision, and be accountable in ways that no AI system currently can be. Whether markets will reliably price these capabilities correctly is another matter — institutional and regulatory choices will shape that outcome as much as technological ones.

The fifty-year horizon resists the language of prediction almost entirely. At this distance, the honest answer is that no one knows which specific capabilities will be economically rewarded, because the structure of the economy itself may be transformed in ways that make current frameworks obsolete. What the historical record does suggest — across the agricultural, industrial, and early digital transitions — is that entirely new categories of valuable work tend to emerge that were not legible in advance. The practical implication is not that long-range planning is pointless, but that the goal of education cannot be to optimize for a specific skill profile that may not exist in the form anticipated.

This brings the question to its most important dimension: how to teach for a future that cannot be fully specified. The research evidence, taken together, points toward a pedagogy organized around durable capacities rather than particular competencies. These include the ability to learn new domains efficiently — what cognitive scientists call "learning to learn" — alongside tolerance for ambiguity, the habit of questioning assumptions, and the capacity to collaborate under conditions of genuine uncertainty. Crucially, deep disciplinary knowledge remains important not as an end in itself but because it provides the cognitive scaffolding from which genuine transfer and creative recombination become possible. Shallow breadth without depth produces workers who are easily displaced; deep knowledge without adaptability produces workers who are brittle.

The pedagogical implication is uncomfortable for systems that prefer measurable outcomes: the capabilities most likely to retain value across all three time horizons are precisely those hardest to assess on a standardized rubric. Teaching for an unpredictable future means accepting that education is, at its core, the cultivation of human judgment — and that judgment, by definition, cannot be fully specified in advance.

How should education respond to the possible decoupling of learning from employment (if AI displaces large categories of work)?

## Learning Beyond Employment: How Education Must Respond to AI-Driven Displacement

The foundational bargain of modern education has always been implicitly economic: study diligently, acquire credentials, and exchange them for productive employment. That bargain is now under serious pressure. The McKinsey Global Institute's 2023 analysis estimated that generative AI could automate tasks accounting for 60 to 70 percent of employees' time across occupations — a projection that dramatically accelerates earlier, more modest forecasts about technological displacement. If that estimate proves even partially correct, the assumption that education exists primarily to prepare workers for labour markets becomes not merely incomplete but potentially obsolete. The question facing educators, policymakers, and institutions is therefore not simply how to update curricula, but whether the entire organising purpose of formal learning requires rethinking from the ground up.

It is worth being precise about what is and is not established here. That AI will automate a significant range of cognitive and routine tasks is well-supported by current evidence. What remains genuinely contested is the pace, the distributional pattern, and the net employment effect of that automation. Historical technological transitions — mechanisation, electrification, computerisation — displaced categories of work while generating new ones, often in ways that contemporaries failed to anticipate. Some economists argue the same dynamic will reassert itself with AI, producing new roles even as old ones disappear. Others contend that generative AI is categorically different because it encroaches on non-routine cognitive work, the domain that previous automation left largely intact. Education cannot wait for that debate to resolve before responding, but it should respond with appropriate epistemic humility rather than assuming any single scenario is certain.

What the scale of potential disruption does demand, regardless of which scenario unfolds, is a serious reconsideration of educational purpose. OECD researchers have argued that if large categories of employment become structurally unavailable, education cannot justify itself solely through the lens of workforce preparation without becoming either irrelevant or cruel — training people for positions that do not exist. This does not mean abandoning vocational and professional preparation; it means refusing to treat it as the sole or even primary rationale for learning. The older humanist tradition — education as the cultivation of judgment, civic capacity, aesthetic sensibility, and the examined life — becomes not a luxury but a practical necessity when the labour market can no longer serve as the principal organising framework for human purpose and social contribution.

Concretely, this reorientation has several implications. Curricula that have been progressively narrowed toward measurable, employment-relevant competencies may need to be deliberately broadened. The humanities, philosophy, and the arts have often been marginalised in recent decades precisely because their labour-market returns are harder to quantify; a world in which AI handles much of the quantifiable cognitive work is one in which those disciplines' contributions to meaning-making, ethical reasoning, and cultural life become more rather than less important. This is an emerging argument rather than a settled consensus, but it has gained traction among educational theorists who see the current moment as an opportunity to correct a decades-long narrowing of educational ambition.

At the same time, education cannot simply retreat into pure humanism and ignore the real transitions workers and learners will face in the near term. The displacement projected by McKinsey and others will not be uniform: it will fall unevenly across sectors, skill levels, and geographies, with workers in routine cognitive roles — data processing, paralegal work, certain categories of writing and analysis — facing the most immediate pressure. Education systems will need to develop genuinely flexible pathways for retraining and lifelong learning, not the thin, underfunded versions that currently exist in most countries, but substantive programmes capable of supporting adults through multiple career transitions across a working life. The tension here is real: institutions built around the formation of young people over long, sequential programmes are structurally ill-suited to the episodic, demand-driven learning that mid-career displacement requires. Resolving that tension will require both institutional redesign and significant public investment.

There is also a deeper question that education alone cannot answer but must engage: if work becomes less central to social identity and economic participation, what replaces it as the primary structure through which people find meaning, status, and community? Education has historically both reflected and reinforced the centrality of productive employment to human dignity. A genuine decoupling would require not just new curricula but new cultural narratives about what a well-lived life looks like — narratives that education systems help to form and transmit. That is a task for which the humanist tradition is better equipped than the vocational one, which is precisely why the current moment, for all its uncertainty, may represent an opportunity to recover educational purposes that instrumental pressures have long suppressed.

The honest answer, then, is that education should respond on multiple fronts simultaneously: preserving and strengthening its capacity to prepare people for the work that remains, while rebuilding its commitment to the broader formation of persons capable of living well in a world where employment is no longer the axis around which everything else turns.

What is the role of education in a society where economic productivity no longer requires most human labor — education for meaning rather than for jobs?

# Education After Work: Meaning, Identity, and the Post-Labor Question

The modern school was built, in large part, as a machine for producing workers. Historians of education such as David Labaree have traced how the "social efficiency" rationale — the idea that schooling's primary purpose is to sort and prepare young people for economic roles — came to dominate 20th-century educational thinking in industrialized nations, gradually displacing older civic and humanistic justifications. That architecture made a certain sense when the economy reliably absorbed most human labor. The question now pressing on educational theory and policy is what happens to the institution when that premise erodes — when automation and artificial intelligence reduce the economy's appetite for human work not at the margins but at the center.

This is not yet a fully realized condition, and intellectual honesty requires acknowledging that. Economists disagree sharply about the pace and depth of labor displacement. Technological optimists argue that new categories of work will emerge, as they have after every previous wave of automation, and that predictions of mass idleness have historically proven wrong. Pessimists counter that the breadth and cognitive reach of contemporary AI systems make this disruption categorically different from the mechanization of physical labor. The honest position is that the outcome remains genuinely uncertain. What is less uncertain is that the question itself is now serious enough to demand that education systems think carefully about purposes they have long neglected.

The most immediate challenge is psychological and existential rather than economic. Across modern societies, paid work has served as the primary source of social identity, daily structure, and felt purpose for most adults. Education has been the institution that prepares people to enter that system of meaning. If the system contracts, education cannot simply continue as credentialing for jobs that may not exist, or exist in far smaller numbers. It would be preparing people for a form of life that is no longer available to them — a profound institutional failure dressed up as continuity.

What might replace this? Scholars working in the tradition of philosophical anthropology and positive psychology have long argued that human flourishing depends on what Aristotle called eudaimonia — a life of active engagement with things that matter, of developed capacities exercised in meaningful ways. Work, in the best cases, has delivered some of this. But work has never been the only vehicle, and for many people in many jobs, it has delivered very little of it. The arts, civic participation, care relationships, intellectual inquiry, spiritual practice, and craft have always been alternative sources of meaning, systematically undervalued precisely because they were not economically productive. An education oriented toward a post-labor world would need to take these seriously as primary rather than supplementary goods.

This reorientation has concrete curricular implications. Philosophy, literature, and the arts — long treated as luxuries or as soft preparation for harder vocational subjects — would move toward the center of educational purpose. Not because they are decorative, but because they are the disciplines most directly concerned with how to live, what to value, and how to make sense of existence under conditions of uncertainty. Civic education, similarly, would need to be reconceived not as preparation for participation in a democracy that happens to have a labor market, but as preparation for collective self-governance in a society that must decide, politically, how to distribute the gains of automated production and how to organize social life when work no longer structures it.

There is a serious tension here that deserves acknowledgment. The turn toward meaning-centered education risks becoming a consolation offered disproportionately to those who have already lost economic security, while the children of the privileged continue to receive education that maintains their competitive position. History offers ample precedent for this pattern: humanistic education has often been the province of elites, while vocational training was directed at everyone else. A genuine reorientation of educational purpose cannot reproduce this division. It would require that education for meaning be understood as a universal entitlement, not a luxury for those who can afford to stop worrying about employment.

There is also the question of what institutions beyond schools would need to change. Education does not operate in isolation. If the broader social architecture continues to tie healthcare, housing security, and social respect to paid employment, then schools oriented toward meaning will be preparing students for a world that does not yet exist — and may feel cruelly irrelevant to students who know they need jobs. Educational transformation of this kind is inseparable from broader political and economic questions about how societies distribute resources and recognize human contribution.

What the scholarship converges on, even amid these tensions, is that the social efficiency rationale has run its course as the organizing principle of education. Whether automation produces mass unemployment or merely transforms the labor market, the reduction of human beings to their economic function has always been an impoverishment. The present moment offers, at considerable cost, an occasion to build something more adequate to the full range of what human life can be.

How do we manage the transition for current workers whose skills are being obsoleted faster than retraining systems can respond?

## When Retraining Runs Slower Than Disruption: Managing the Transition for Workers Whose Skills Are Being Obsoleted by AI

The central difficulty facing workforce policy today is not simply that jobs are changing — it is that they are changing faster than the institutions designed to respond to such change can move. The World Economic Forum's Future of Jobs Report 2023 documents a striking compression in the useful life of professional skills: from an estimated ten to fifteen years in the 1980s down to roughly five years today, with some technical competencies in software development and data analytics becoming obsolete in as little as two to three years. The same report projects that 44% of workers' core skills will be disrupted within the current decade. Traditional education and retraining systems, built around multi-year curricula, accreditation cycles, and institutional planning horizons measured in semesters rather than quarters, were never designed to operate at this tempo. The mismatch is structural, not incidental, and closing it requires rethinking the architecture of workforce transition rather than simply accelerating existing mechanisms.

The scale of the problem is uneven in ways that matter enormously for policy. Displacement is not distributed randomly across the workforce. Workers in routine cognitive roles — data entry, paralegal research, certain categories of financial analysis, customer service — face the sharpest near-term exposure, and these roles are disproportionately held by workers in mid-career who lack both the time horizon of younger workers and the accumulated capital of senior professionals. For a 45-year-old whose expertise has been rendered redundant, a two-year retraining programme represents a significant gamble on an uncertain outcome, undertaken at a moment of financial and psychological stress. The calculus is very different from that facing a 22-year-old choosing a first career path.

One response that has gained traction among employers and policymakers is the shift toward modular, stackable credentials — short-form certifications that can be completed in weeks or months rather than years, and that can be combined incrementally into recognised qualification pathways. Platforms such as Coursera, edX, and various employer-led academies have expanded this space considerably. The evidence on their effectiveness is genuinely mixed, however. Completion rates for self-directed online learning remain low, particularly among workers who are already employed full-time or managing caregiving responsibilities. There is also a persistent credentialing problem: many employers continue to weight traditional degrees heavily in hiring decisions, meaning that a stack of micro-credentials may not translate into the labour market mobility it promises on paper. This gap between credential supply and employer recognition is one of the more contested areas in current workforce research.

A more promising structural intervention, supported by evidence from several national contexts, is the integration of retraining support directly into employment rather than treating it as something that happens after job loss. Germany's Kurzarbeit model, which subsidises reduced working hours during downturns to preserve employment relationships while workers retrain, has demonstrated that keeping workers attached to employers during transitions significantly improves outcomes compared to retraining the unemployed. Singapore's SkillsFuture programme takes a related approach, providing individual learning accounts that workers can draw on continuously throughout their careers, normalising upskilling as an ongoing activity rather than a crisis response. Neither model translates perfectly to other institutional contexts, but both point toward the same underlying principle: transitions managed within employment are substantially less costly — economically and psychologically — than transitions managed through unemployment.

The role of employers themselves is underappreciated in most policy discussions, which tend to focus on public education systems. There is reasonably strong evidence that firms which invest in internal mobility — actively redeploying workers whose roles are being automated into adjacent functions rather than simply eliminating positions — achieve better outcomes for both workers and the organisation. Amazon's much-publicised Upskilling 2025 commitment and similar corporate programmes represent one end of this spectrum, though critics note that such initiatives often reach a relatively small proportion of affected workers and tend to favour those already closest to technical roles. The honest assessment is that employer-led retraining works well for workers who are already relatively skilled and works poorly for those at the bottom of the internal labour market.

What remains genuinely unresolved is the question of income support during transition. Even well-designed retraining programmes require workers to sustain themselves financially while learning, and existing unemployment insurance systems in most countries were designed for short-term frictional unemployment rather than the longer structural transitions now becoming common. Proposals for extended transition accounts, portable benefits, or more ambitious income floor mechanisms remain politically contested and empirically underexplored at scale. The evidence base for what adequate income support during retraining actually costs, and what it produces in terms of employment outcomes, is thinner than policymakers would wish.

The honest conclusion is that no single intervention closes the gap between the pace of skill obsolescence and the pace of retraining systems. What the evidence supports is a portfolio approach: modular credentials combined with employer recognition reform, retraining embedded in employment rather than following job loss, stronger income support during transitions, and continuous learning cultures normalised well before disruption arrives. The window for building these systems proactively is narrowing.

How does AI in education affect democracy — the formation of citizens capable of judgment, deliberation, and resistance to manipulation?

## The Algorithmic Citizen: AI in Education and the Future of Democratic Life

Democracy has always depended on something more than voting. It requires citizens capable of forming independent judgments, engaging seriously with views unlike their own, recognizing manipulation when it appears, and tolerating the discomfort of genuine disagreement. These capacities are not innate; they are cultivated — historically, through education. The rapid integration of artificial intelligence into educational systems therefore raises a question that goes well beyond pedagogy: what kind of minds, and what kind of citizens, does AI-mediated learning tend to produce?

The most thoroughly documented concern centres on what researchers call epistemic narrowing. Personalization algorithms — the engines that drive adaptive tutoring systems and AI-curated content platforms — are typically optimized for measurable outcomes: engagement rates, test performance, time-on-task. These are not trivial goods, but they are not the same as the goods democracy requires. When a system learns that a student responds well to certain framings, certain difficulty levels, certain ideological registers, and then systematically serves more of the same, it may be producing efficient learners while quietly producing intellectually narrower ones. The deliberative citizen that democratic theory imagines is someone who has genuinely encountered opposing arguments, sat with uncomfortable evidence, and been forced to revise or defend a position under pressure. An AI tutor optimized for smooth progress has little structural incentive to provide any of that friction.

This concern is serious, but it must be held carefully. The research establishing epistemic narrowing as a demonstrated harm in educational AI specifically — as opposed to a plausible extrapolation from the well-documented filter-bubble effects of social media recommendation systems — remains at an early stage. The analogy to social media is suggestive rather than conclusive. Educational platforms operate under different incentive structures, involve different kinds of content, and are used in contexts — classrooms, curricula, teacher oversight — that may interrupt algorithmic tendencies in ways that consumer platforms do not. Whether the narrowing effect is as pronounced, or as democratically consequential, in educational settings as in news and social media environments is genuinely contested.

A second and related concern involves the formation of epistemic autonomy — the capacity to evaluate sources, weigh evidence, and reach conclusions through one's own reasoning rather than deference to authority. Here the worry runs in a somewhat different direction. If students routinely receive AI-generated answers, summaries, and explanations, and if those outputs are experienced as authoritative and reliable, there is a plausible risk that the habit of independent inquiry atrophies. Democratic citizenship requires not just access to correct information but the practiced ability to assess information oneself. A student who has learned to trust the AI's answer may be less equipped to recognize when an authoritative-seeming source — whether an AI system, a politician, or a media outlet — is wrong, partial, or deliberately misleading.

This connects directly to the question of manipulation resistance. One of the most pressing democratic challenges of the present moment is the proliferation of sophisticated disinformation, including AI-generated disinformation. The capacity to resist such manipulation is partly a matter of media literacy, but it is also a matter of epistemic character — habits of skepticism, comfort with uncertainty, and experience of having been wrong and corrected. If AI educational tools systematically smooth away the experience of productive failure and intellectual struggle, they may inadvertently produce citizens who are more, not less, vulnerable to confident-sounding falsehoods.

Against these concerns, a more optimistic reading is possible and deserves acknowledgment. AI tutoring systems, at their best, can expose students to a far wider range of material than any single teacher or textbook could provide. They can be designed — and some are being designed — to present multiple perspectives on contested questions, to flag the difference between established consensus and live debate, and to prompt students to articulate and defend their own reasoning. The technology is not inherently narrowing; it depends entirely on the values embedded in its design. An AI system built around the goals of democratic education, rather than engagement metrics, could in principle strengthen rather than weaken deliberative capacity.

The difficulty is that the incentive structures currently governing educational AI development do not reliably point in that direction. Platforms are built by companies with commercial interests; adoption decisions are made by institutions under pressure to demonstrate measurable improvements in test scores. The democratic purposes of education — forming judgment, cultivating tolerance for complexity, building resistance to manipulation — are real but hard to quantify, and hard-to-quantify goods tend to lose out in optimization processes.

What the evidence, taken together, suggests is not that AI in education is democratically harmful by nature, but that it carries genuine democratic risks that are not being systematically addressed. The question of what kind of citizen AI-mediated education produces is not yet being asked loudly enough — and in a democracy, that silence is itself a problem worth examining.

What shared human culture and values should education transmit when AI fragments information into personalized realities?

## The Common World at Risk: Education, AI Personalization, and the Transmission of Shared Culture

The question of what education should transmit has always been contested, but it has rarely been as urgent as it is now. When artificial intelligence systems tailor information environments to individual preferences, they do not merely customize learning — they risk dissolving the shared epistemic ground on which democratic culture, moral reasoning, and civic life depend. The challenge for education is therefore not simply pedagogical but civilizational: how to sustain a common world when the dominant technologies of knowledge distribution are pulling steadily in the opposite direction.

The empirical case for concern begins with what researchers call the filter bubble problem. Eli Pariser's foundational work demonstrated that algorithmic personalization systematically narrows the information environments individuals encounter, reducing their exposure to divergent or challenging viewpoints. A landmark 2015 study by Bakshy, Messing, and Adamic, published in Science, examined the behavior of millions of Facebook users and found that algorithmic curation meaningfully reduced the cross-cutting content people saw, even when such content was available in their networks. The study's findings are contested in their precise magnitude — some researchers argue that individual self-selection plays a larger role than the algorithm itself — but the directional conclusion is robust: personalization tends toward epistemic enclosure. When AI tutoring systems, content platforms, and recommendation engines are applied to education, they import this same logic. A student whose learning environment is optimized for engagement and comfort will encounter fewer of the dissonant ideas, unfamiliar traditions, and morally complex texts that education has historically used to expand rather than confirm the self.

This creates a structural tension at the heart of contemporary schooling. The efficiency gains from AI personalization are real: adaptive systems can identify gaps in understanding, adjust pacing, and sustain motivation in ways that uniform instruction cannot. But efficiency in the transmission of skills is not the same as education in the fuller sense. The Western tradition of liberal education, and its analogues in other cultures, has always insisted that schooling must do something more than optimize individual performance — it must initiate the young into a shared inheritance. That inheritance includes not only canonical texts and historical narratives but the habits of mind required to engage with people whose formation has been different from one's own.

The question of which shared culture education should transmit is, of course, genuinely contested. Critics of traditional canons have rightly pointed out that what presents itself as universal culture has often been the culture of dominant groups, and that the exclusion of other traditions from the curriculum has carried real costs for students whose histories were rendered invisible. This debate is not resolved by the arrival of AI; if anything, personalization sharpens it. An AI system trained on existing cultural hierarchies will reproduce them; one designed to reflect each student's existing cultural context risks reinforcing parochialism rather than expanding it. Neither outcome is satisfactory.

What the research suggests, however, is that the answer cannot be the abandonment of shared content in favor of purely individualized pathways. Societies require what the philosopher Hannah Arendt called a "common world" — a set of shared references, stories, and values that make genuine public conversation possible. Without some common curriculum, students from different backgrounds have no meeting ground, no shared vocabulary for disagreement, no basis for the kind of mutual recognition that democratic life requires. The transmission of this common world is precisely what AI-driven personalization, left unchecked, tends to erode.

Education's response must therefore be deliberate and structural. Several principles emerge from the evidence. First, shared texts and experiences — literature, history, scientific reasoning, ethical philosophy — should remain a non-negotiable core of schooling, not because any particular canon is beyond criticism, but because the act of studying the same things together is itself educationally significant. Second, the curriculum should explicitly include the skills of navigating disagreement: media literacy, the evaluation of sources, the capacity to engage charitably with opposing arguments. These are not merely technical competencies but civic virtues, and they are precisely what filter-bubble environments fail to cultivate. Third, educators and policymakers must scrutinize the design of AI systems used in schools, asking not only whether they improve test scores but whether they expose students to the breadth of human experience or narrow it.

None of this requires rejecting the genuine benefits of adaptive technology. It requires insisting that personalization serve educational ends rather than define them. The deepest values education must transmit — intellectual honesty, respect for evidence, the capacity for empathy across difference, and a sense of obligation to a world larger than oneself — are not values that any algorithm will spontaneously generate. They must be chosen, defended, and taught. That act of choosing is itself a cultural and moral commitment, and it belongs to human educators, not to the systems they deploy.

Looking back from 2050: what decisions made in this decade about AI and education will we most regret or celebrate — and how do we choose wisely now?

## The Decisions That Will Define Learning: A View from the Future

History tends to reveal the significance of choices only after the window for making them differently has closed. The introduction of the printing press, the design of mass schooling systems, the arrival of television in classrooms — each moment seemed, to those living through it, like a practical problem of implementation. Only in retrospect did it become clear that something more fundamental was being decided: who would have access to knowledge, on whose terms, and with what consequences for human development. The rapid integration of artificial intelligence into education in the 2020s belongs to this category of hinge moments, and the decisions being made now — about infrastructure, pedagogy, governance, and purpose — will almost certainly be the ones that a 2050 vantage point judges most harshly or most admiringly.

The most consequential and least glamorous of these decisions concerns infrastructure and access. A 2023 UNESCO report documented that approximately 2.9 billion people worldwide still lack reliable internet connectivity, which means that AI-powered tutoring systems, adaptive learning platforms, and intelligent feedback tools — however sophisticated — remain entirely inaccessible to a substantial portion of humanity. The decisions made in this decade about whether to treat connectivity as a prerequisite for educational AI deployment, or to proceed with rollout in well-resourced contexts while assuming access will eventually follow, will create path dependencies that persist for generations. Educational technology has a well-documented history of amplifying existing inequalities rather than dissolving them: the schools and systems that adopt AI tools earliest and most effectively tend to be those already advantaged by funding, teacher training, and institutional capacity. If the 2020s are remembered as a decade in which AI made excellent education dramatically better for those who already had it, while leaving the connectivity gap unaddressed, that will constitute a serious and foreseeable failure — one that cannot be attributed to ignorance, since the evidence is available now.

The equity question is not only geographic. Within connected societies, the design choices embedded in AI educational systems carry their own distributional consequences. Algorithmic systems trained predominantly on data from certain linguistic, cultural, and socioeconomic contexts will perform unevenly across populations, and the students most likely to be harmed by those performance gaps are those already underserved by conventional schooling. This is an area where the research is still developing, and where honest uncertainty is warranted: the magnitude of bias effects in educational AI, and the degree to which they can be mitigated through careful design and diverse training data, remains genuinely contested. What is not contested is that these questions require active attention rather than passive optimism.

A second cluster of decisions concerns the relationship between AI and the cognitive development of learners. There is a real and unresolved tension in the research between two legitimate goods. On one side, AI tutoring systems have demonstrated genuine promise in providing personalized, patient, and responsive instruction — particularly for students who lack access to skilled human tutors. On the other side, there are well-grounded concerns that over-reliance on AI assistance during the learning process may impede the development of precisely the capacities — sustained effort, tolerance for confusion, independent problem-solving — that education is meant to cultivate. The analogy to calculators is instructive but imperfect: calculators offload computation while leaving mathematical reasoning intact, whereas AI writing and reasoning assistants potentially offload the very cognitive processes that education is trying to build. How schools navigate this tension — whether they develop principled frameworks for when AI assistance supports learning and when it substitutes for it — will matter enormously. A 2050 retrospective might celebrate this decade for having worked out those distinctions carefully, or regret it for having allowed convenience to foreclose the question.

Governance and accountability represent a third domain of consequential choice. The companies developing AI educational tools are making decisions about data collection, algorithmic design, and commercial incentives that will shape learning environments for millions of children, largely outside democratic deliberation. The degree to which governments, educators, and civil society establish meaningful oversight — not merely formal regulation, but genuine capacity to evaluate, challenge, and redirect these systems — will determine whether AI in education serves educational purposes or educational purposes come to serve AI deployment.

Choosing wisely in this moment requires resisting two symmetrical errors: the uncritical enthusiasm that treats AI as an unambiguous good requiring only faster adoption, and the reflexive resistance that treats any technological change in education as a threat to humanistic values. The more demanding and more honest path is to insist on specificity — which students, which learning goals, which contexts, with what evidence — and to keep the question of educational purpose primary. The technology is a means. The question of what human flourishing through learning actually requires is the one that must not be delegated to the systems being built to serve it.

Chapter 7. Chapter 7: The Evidence Base — What Research Actually Shows

What does current peer-reviewed evidence show about the effects of AI tutoring systems on learning outcomes, and how strong is that evidence?

## AI Tutoring Systems and Learning Outcomes: What the Evidence Actually Shows

The promise of artificial intelligence in education rests substantially on a body of research into intelligent tutoring systems — software that adapts instructional content to individual learners in real time. Decades of peer-reviewed work, culminating in several meta-analyses, suggest these systems can meaningfully improve learning outcomes. Yet the strength of that evidence is uneven, and the gap between controlled research conditions and ordinary classroom practice remains one of the field's most persistent problems.

The most comprehensive quantitative picture comes from meta-analytic work. A 2023 meta-analysis by Kulik and Fletcher, extending a lineage of synthesis research that Kulik and Kulik began in 1991, examined a large body of intelligent tutoring system studies and found average effect sizes ranging from 0.3 to 0.8 standard deviations compared to conventional classroom instruction. In educational research, where an effect size of 0.4 is often treated as a meaningful threshold, these figures are notable. An effect at the upper end of that range would, in practical terms, represent something like moving a student from the fiftieth to the seventy-ninth percentile — a substantial gain. Even the lower bound suggests a reliably positive, if modest, advantage over traditional teaching.

These numbers, however, require careful interpretation. Meta-analyses aggregate studies that vary enormously in design quality, and the intelligent tutoring literature is no exception. Many of the studies contributing to these effect sizes were conducted under controlled or semi-controlled conditions — often with researcher involvement, motivated volunteer participants, short intervention windows, and outcome measures closely aligned to the content the tutoring system actually taught. These are the conditions most likely to produce strong results, and they do not straightforwardly represent what happens when a system is deployed at scale in a typical school, with ordinary students, distracted teachers, and the full complexity of institutional life.

This is what researchers call the "lab-to-classroom gap," and it is not a minor technical quibble. Systems that perform impressively in tightly managed pilots have repeatedly shown attenuated effects — sometimes approaching zero — when evaluated in large-scale randomised controlled trials conducted in authentic school settings. The history of educational technology is littered with interventions that looked transformative in early studies and disappointing in broader deployment. Intelligent tutoring systems are not immune to this pattern, and the honest reading of the evidence must hold both the positive meta-analytic signal and this sobering contextual caveat simultaneously.

Subject domain matters considerably. The strongest and most consistent evidence for AI tutoring effectiveness comes from mathematics and, to a lesser extent, procedural domains in science. Systems like Carnegie Learning's MATHia have been evaluated in large-scale studies with reasonably rigorous designs and have shown durable positive effects on algebra achievement. Reading and writing present a more complicated picture: the adaptive feedback loops that work well for solving equations are harder to engineer for the interpretation of literary texts or the development of argumentative prose, and the evidence in these domains is thinner and more contested.

The question of which students benefit most is also unresolved. Some studies suggest that AI tutoring systems are particularly effective for students who are already moderately engaged and have sufficient prior knowledge to make use of adaptive feedback — in other words, that the systems work best for those who are already reasonably well served by conventional instruction. Other research points in a more optimistic direction, finding that students who struggle most with traditional pacing benefit from the patience and individualisation that a well-designed system can provide. These findings are not necessarily contradictory — different systems, different populations, and different implementation contexts may genuinely produce different patterns — but the field has not yet produced a settled account of for whom these tools work best.

The emergence of large language model-based tutoring tools since 2022 introduces a further complication. These systems differ architecturally from the rule-based and Bayesian intelligent tutoring systems that dominate the existing research literature, and the peer-reviewed evidence base for their educational effects is, at present, extremely thin. Early studies are appearing, but they are mostly small, short-term, and methodologically preliminary. Enthusiasm in the popular and commercial press has run well ahead of the evidence, and it would be a mistake to assume that the positive findings accumulated for earlier intelligent tutoring systems transfer automatically to large language model tutors, which have different failure modes — including confident confabulation — that may be particularly consequential in educational settings.

What the current peer-reviewed evidence supports, then, is a qualified optimism. Intelligent tutoring systems, particularly in mathematics and procedural subjects, can produce meaningful learning gains under the right conditions. The effect sizes in meta-analyses are real and not trivial. But the evidence base is strongest in conditions that differ from typical school deployment, the benefits are not uniformly distributed across subjects or student populations, and the newest generation of AI tutoring tools remains largely unevaluated by rigorous independent research. The field knows enough to take these systems seriously; it does not yet know enough to deploy them uncritically.

What does research reveal about how students actually use generative AI when unsupervised — as distinct from how they report using it?

## What Students Actually Do With Generative AI: The Gap Between Report and Reality

One of the more uncomfortable truths emerging from educational research on generative AI is that students do not accurately describe how they use it. This is not a minor discrepancy of degree — the gap between self-reported behaviour and observed behaviour is, in several studies, categorical. Students who characterise their AI use as occasional or supplementary are, when their actual digital activity is logged, using tools like ChatGPT for the majority of their writing assignments, frequently pasting in entire prompts and accepting or lightly editing the output. The methodological implication is significant: survey-based research, which constitutes the bulk of the existing literature on student AI use, may be systematically misleading.

The most direct evidence comes from behavioural logging studies, in which browser extensions or platform-level trace data capture what students actually do rather than what they later recall or choose to disclose. Research conducted at a major research university using this approach found a consistent pattern: self-reported "occasional" users were, in practice, heavy users whose engagement with AI tools was woven into the fabric of their written work. This kind of finding is not easily explained away by faulty memory. The gap is too large and too directional — students underreport, not overreport — which points toward social desirability bias, institutional anxiety about academic integrity policies, or a genuine cognitive dissonance in how students categorise their own behaviour.

That last possibility deserves attention. There is emerging evidence that many students do not experience AI-assisted writing as "using AI" in any morally or academically significant sense. When a student pastes a paragraph prompt into ChatGPT, reads the output, and then rewrites portions of it, they may genuinely perceive this as a form of drafting or brainstorming rather than as outsourcing. The taxonomy students apply to their own behaviour does not map cleanly onto the categories that institutions use when writing academic integrity policies. This is not merely a rationalisation after the fact; it appears to reflect a sincere, if contested, understanding of what constitutes original work in an age of AI assistance.

The pattern of actual use, as revealed by trace data, tends to cluster around a few dominant behaviours. Whole-assignment generation — submitting AI output with minimal modification — exists but is less common than a more hybrid practice in which AI produces a structural scaffold or initial draft that the student then edits to varying degrees. Students also use generative AI extensively for tasks that fall outside traditional definitions of academic dishonesty: generating practice questions, explaining concepts in alternative terms, checking reasoning, and summarising readings. These uses are rarely captured in self-report surveys that frame AI use primarily in terms of assignment completion, which means the surveys miss a substantial portion of actual behaviour while simultaneously undercounting the more problematic uses.

There is a meaningful tension in the research between those who interpret this behavioural data as evidence of widespread academic misconduct and those who argue it reflects a rational adaptation to tools that are now ambient features of intellectual life. The former position draws on studies showing that students who rely heavily on AI-generated text demonstrate weaker retention of course material and reduced ability to perform on unassisted assessments — suggesting that the cognitive work associated with learning is being bypassed rather than augmented. The latter position notes that similar anxieties accompanied the introduction of calculators, search engines, and spell-checkers, and that the relevant question is not whether students use AI but whether they are developing the capacity to use it critically and productively.

What the behavioural data cannot easily resolve is the question of intent and awareness. Some students using AI heavily appear to know they are circumventing institutional expectations and are making a calculated choice. Others seem genuinely uncertain about where the line is, particularly in institutions where policies are vague, inconsistent, or recently changed. This ambiguity is itself a finding: unsupervised AI use does not occur in a normative vacuum, but the norms are contested and poorly communicated, which shapes behaviour in ways that self-report instruments are ill-equipped to detect.

What is established, then, is that self-report data substantially underestimates both the frequency and the depth of student AI use. What remains genuinely open is how to interpret that use — whether it represents a crisis of academic integrity, an unguided but potentially productive adaptation, or some irreducible mixture of both depending on the student, the task, and the institution. The methodological lesson is clear: understanding what students actually do with generative AI requires observational and trace-based methods, not surveys alone. The pedagogical and policy lessons are considerably harder to draw, and the research is only beginning to catch up with the behaviour it is trying to describe.

What is currently known from cognitive science about AI's effects on memory formation, retention, and transfer of learning?

## Memory, Machines, and the Learner's Mind: What Cognitive Science Currently Knows About AI's Effects on Learning

The arrival of conversational AI in educational settings has sharpened a question that cognitive scientists were already wrestling with in the age of search engines: what happens to human memory when an extraordinarily capable external system stands ready to supply whatever information a person might need? The evidence accumulated so far is neither uniformly alarming nor reassuring. It points instead to a set of genuine mechanisms, some well-established and some still contested, through which AI tools reshape the processes of encoding, retaining, and transferring knowledge.

The most firmly grounded starting point is Betsy Sparrow's 2011 research on what she termed the "Google Effect." Sparrow and her colleagues demonstrated that when people expect information to remain externally available, they invest less cognitive effort in encoding it into long-term memory. The brain, it appears, makes a pragmatic calculation: if retrieval from an outside source is easy and reliable, the metabolic cost of consolidating information internally seems unnecessary. Contemporary cognitive scientists have extended this logic directly to AI assistants, which are considerably more capable, more fluent, and more immediately responsive than a search engine returning a list of links. Where Google required the user to read, evaluate, and synthesise results, a large language model delivers a composed answer. The offloading, in other words, can now be more complete, and the concern is that encoding may be correspondingly shallower.

This matters because of what cognitive science has established about the relationship between memory and understanding. Working memory — the limited mental workspace in which active thinking occurs — depends on long-term memory to supply the schemas, concepts, and prior knowledge that give new information meaning. When a learner has not encoded foundational material, each encounter with a related problem demands more from working memory, leaving less capacity for the reasoning that produces genuine comprehension. The implication is that habitual AI-assisted retrieval, if it displaces the effortful processing that drives encoding, could quietly erode the very knowledge structures that make complex thought possible.

Yet the picture is more complicated than a straightforward warning against AI use. Cognitive load theory, developed by John Sweller and refined over decades, holds that offloading routine or lower-order tasks can free working memory for higher-order reasoning. A student who uses AI to handle mechanical aspects of a problem — formatting, basic calculation, surface-level information retrieval — might, in principle, direct more cognitive resources toward analysis, synthesis, and evaluation. Whether this benefit materialises depends critically on how the tool is used. Passive consumption of AI-generated answers is cognitively very different from using AI as a Socratic interlocutor that prompts the learner to explain, question, and elaborate. The former risks bypassing encoding altogether; the latter may actually support it.

Transfer of learning — the ability to apply knowledge acquired in one context to a novel situation — is where the stakes become highest and the evidence most unsettled. Transfer is notoriously difficult to achieve even under optimal instructional conditions, and it depends heavily on the depth and organisation of knowledge in long-term memory. Learners who have genuinely encoded and repeatedly retrieved material develop flexible, interconnected representations that can be mapped onto new problems. Learners who have relied on external systems for retrieval tend to have thinner, more context-dependent representations. The concern, not yet fully tested in AI-specific research but well-supported by the broader literature on desirable difficulties, is that AI assistance may reduce the retrieval practice and productive struggle that are known to strengthen transferable knowledge.

Retrieval practice itself deserves emphasis here, because it is one of the most robust findings in memory research. The act of recalling information — not merely re-reading or re-exposing oneself to it — substantially strengthens long-term retention and transfer. Spaced repetition compounds this effect. AI tutoring systems that incorporate these principles, prompting learners to retrieve and apply knowledge rather than simply presenting correct answers, have shown promise in early studies. The critical variable, then, is not whether AI is present but whether its design and use preserve or circumvent the cognitive processes that consolidation requires.

What remains genuinely open is the question of individual and developmental differences. Younger learners, whose knowledge bases are still forming, may be more vulnerable to the encoding deficits associated with heavy offloading than expert learners who already possess rich schemas. Experts can use AI to extend their capabilities without sacrificing the underlying knowledge structures they have already built; novices may not have that foundation to fall back on.

The honest summary of the current state of knowledge is this: the cognitive mechanisms by which AI could impair memory formation and transfer are well-understood and plausible; the extent to which they operate in real educational settings, and under what conditions they can be mitigated by thoughtful design, remains an active and urgent area of inquiry.

What does the evidence show about AI's impact on writing skill development, from early literacy through academic and professional writing?

## Writing in the Age of Intelligent Machines: What the Evidence Shows

Writing is not merely a means of recording thought; it is, in large measure, how thought is formed. The cognitive labour of planning an argument, wrestling with a draft, and revising toward clarity is not incidental to writing development — it is the mechanism by which that development occurs. This is why the arrival of generative AI tools capable of producing fluent prose on demand raises questions that go well beyond academic integrity. The central question is whether AI assistance accelerates writing skill or quietly erodes it.

The most consistent signal in the current research points toward risk rather than benefit when AI tools are used as substitutes for compositional effort. A 2023 study published in Computers & Education found that students who relied heavily on AI-generated text showed significantly less engagement with the cognitive processes — planning, drafting, revision — that writing researchers have long identified as the engines of skill acquisition. This is not a surprising finding in theoretical terms. Cognitive load theory and the broader literature on deliberate practice both predict that offloading effortful processes to an external agent will reduce the internal development those processes would otherwise produce. What the AI research is beginning to confirm empirically is that this prediction holds in writing contexts specifically.

The concern is sharpest at the earlier stages of development. For young writers still acquiring foundational literacy — learning to translate thought into sentence structure, to manage the physical and cognitive demands of composition simultaneously — the risk of premature scaffolding is particularly acute. Early writing development depends on repeated, effortful practice with the full composing process. Tools that short-circuit that process, even with good intentions, may deprive novice writers of the very struggle that builds competence. The evidence here remains somewhat preliminary, since most empirical work has focused on secondary and post-secondary students rather than early childhood settings, but the theoretical grounds for concern are well-established.

At the secondary and undergraduate level, where the bulk of current research is concentrated, the picture is more nuanced. Students who use AI tools to generate complete drafts show the clearest signs of skill stagnation, but the effects of more targeted AI assistance — feedback on structure, suggestions for revision, help with sentence-level clarity — are considerably less settled. Some researchers argue that AI-generated feedback, when it prompts students to make their own revisions rather than simply accepting corrections, can function as a form of guided practice that supports development. Others counter that even feedback-oriented AI use tends, in practice, to collapse into passive acceptance, with students adopting suggestions without the metacognitive engagement that makes feedback educationally valuable. Both positions have empirical support, and the honest conclusion is that outcomes depend heavily on how tools are integrated into instruction and what habits of use students develop.

The professional writing context introduces a different set of considerations. For experienced writers — those who have already developed robust compositional skills — AI assistance appears to function more as a productivity tool than a developmental risk. When a skilled writer uses AI to generate a rough structure, overcome a blank page, or check for inconsistencies, the underlying cognitive architecture of their writing ability is not threatened in the same way it would be for a novice. The skill is already internalized; the tool is augmenting rather than replacing it. This distinction between novice and expert use is underappreciated in public debate, which tends to treat AI writing assistance as uniformly beneficial or uniformly harmful.

What the evidence does not yet resolve is the long-term trajectory. Most studies are short-term, measuring outcomes over a semester or less. Whether students who use AI heavily throughout their education arrive at adulthood with meaningfully weaker writing abilities than those who did not is a question that longitudinal research has not yet answered. There is also a live debate about whether the skills that matter most in writing are themselves changing — whether the ability to prompt, evaluate, and refine AI-generated text constitutes a new form of compositional competence that educators should be cultivating rather than suppressing. This is a genuinely open question, and intellectually honest engagement with the evidence requires acknowledging it as such.

What can be said with reasonable confidence is this: AI tools used as replacements for the effortful cognitive work of writing are likely to impede skill development, particularly in novice writers; AI tools used as targeted supplements, within instructional frameworks that preserve the essential labour of composition, may be compatible with development and in some cases supportive of it. The difference lies not in the technology but in the pedagogy surrounding it — a finding that, in retrospect, should surprise no one familiar with the history of educational technology.
