What documented harms — from data breaches to well-being incidents to learning setbacks — have occurred in real AI deployments, and what changed as a result?

## When AI Harms Students: Documented Incidents and Their Aftermath

The history of artificial intelligence in education is not only a story of promise. Alongside genuine pedagogical gains, real deployments have produced real harms — to student privacy, psychological well-being, academic fairness, and learning itself. Examining these incidents carefully, and tracing what changed in their wake, offers a more honest account of where the technology has succeeded and where it has failed.

The most extensively documented category of harm emerged from the rapid, largely unvetted adoption of AI-powered proctoring tools during the COVID-19 pandemic. Platforms such as Proctorio, ExamSoft, and ProctorU were deployed at hundreds of universities within weeks, as institutions scrambled to move assessment online. The Electronic Frontier Foundation documented in 2020 that these systems collected extraordinarily invasive data — continuous video and audio feeds, eye-tracking patterns, keystroke dynamics, browser histories, and in some cases full scans of students' physical environments — often without meaningful informed consent and with opaque data-retention policies. Students had little clarity on who could access their recordings, for how long, or under what legal circumstances.

The bias dimension of these tools proved particularly serious. Multiple investigations found that facial recognition and gaze-detection algorithms performed significantly worse on students with darker skin tones, a disparity consistent with broader findings on racial bias in computer vision systems. Students of colour reported being repeatedly flagged as suspicious, required to reposition themselves under bright lights, or locked out of examinations entirely when the software failed to verify their identity. Disabled students faced compounding difficulties: those with conditions affecting eye movement, motor control, or attention were disproportionately flagged for behaviours the algorithms interpreted as cheating. The psychological toll was substantial and well-attested. Surveys conducted at multiple institutions found elevated anxiety levels among students using these platforms compared with those assessed by other means, with some students reporting panic attacks during examinations and others describing the experience as dehumanising. Student unions at universities in the United Kingdom, Canada, and Australia formally protested the tools, and several institutions faced organised campaigns demanding their removal.

The consequences were meaningful, if uneven. A number of universities — including some in the Netherlands following a court challenge — discontinued mandatory AI proctoring or restricted its use to voluntary opt-in arrangements. Several student governments successfully lobbied for the right to take examinations in supervised physical settings as an alternative. The incidents also accelerated regulatory scrutiny: the UK's Information Commissioner's Office and equivalent bodies in the European Union began examining whether these deployments complied with data protection law, and some vendors were compelled to revise their data-handling practices. What did not change uniformly was institutional procurement behaviour; many universities continued using these tools, and the episode illustrated how quickly educational technology can be adopted at scale before adequate safeguards exist.

Data breach incidents represent a second documented category of harm. Student data held by edtech platforms has proven an attractive target, and several breaches have exposed sensitive records — including learning profiles, behavioural assessments, and in some cases mental health disclosures made through AI-assisted counselling tools. The 2021 breach affecting Illuminate Education in the United States exposed the records of over a million students across multiple school districts, including special education data and disciplinary histories. The harm in such cases is not always immediately visible, but the exposure of detailed behavioural and academic profiles creates long-term risks, particularly for minors whose records may persist and be misused years later. In response, several US states strengthened their student privacy statutes, and the incident renewed calls for stricter limits on what data edtech vendors may collect and retain.

Learning setbacks attributable to AI systems are harder to document with precision, partly because causation is difficult to isolate. There is, however, credible evidence that poorly calibrated adaptive learning platforms have in some cases directed students into remedial loops that were difficult to exit, effectively slowing rather than accelerating progress. When algorithmic recommendations carry significant weight in determining what content a student receives, errors in the model can compound over time. This concern remains more contested than the proctoring harms — it is genuinely difficult to distinguish a platform's failure from other variables affecting student performance — but it has prompted calls for greater human oversight of algorithmic recommendations and clearer mechanisms for students and teachers to override system decisions.

Taken together, these incidents reveal a consistent pattern: harms have tended to occur when AI tools were deployed rapidly, at scale, without adequate pre-deployment evaluation, and in contexts where affected students had little power to refuse or seek redress. The changes that followed — revised policies, regulatory investigations, some institutional withdrawals — represent genuine responses, but they have been reactive rather than anticipatory. The more durable lesson is structural: the speed of adoption in education has repeatedly outpaced the development of the governance frameworks needed to make that adoption safe.

Chapter 9. Chapter 9: Policy and Governance in Practice

Which national and regional AI-in-education regulations currently exist, and how do their approaches compare?

## Governing the Classroom Algorithm: National and Regional Approaches to AI-in-Education Regulation

The arrival of sophisticated AI systems in schools and universities has prompted governments around the world to reach for regulatory tools, but the instruments they have chosen differ markedly in their legal force, philosophical assumptions, and practical scope. The result is a patchwork of frameworks that ranges from binding supranational law to voluntary guidance, with significant consequences for how educational institutions design, procure, and deploy AI.

The most architecturally ambitious response has come from the European Union. The AI Act (Regulation 2024/1689), which entered into force in August 2024, is the world's first comprehensive binding legal framework for artificial intelligence, and it treats education as a domain of elevated concern. Under Annex III of the Act, AI systems used in education and vocational training are classified as high-risk when they determine or materially influence access to educational institutions, assess students, or monitor behaviour during learning. This classification is not merely symbolic. High-risk systems must satisfy demanding requirements before deployment: conformity assessments, detailed technical documentation, human oversight mechanisms, transparency obligations toward affected individuals, and registration in a publicly accessible EU database. Providers and deployers — including schools and universities that configure general-purpose tools for high-stakes purposes — bear legal responsibility for compliance. The Act also prohibits certain AI practices outright, including real-time remote biometric identification in public spaces and systems that exploit psychological vulnerabilities, prohibitions that carry direct implications for surveillance-oriented edtech products.

The EU's approach reflects a rights-based philosophy rooted in the Charter of Fundamental Rights and a precautionary instinct: where AI touches consequential decisions about people's educational futures, the burden of proof falls on those deploying the technology to demonstrate safety and fairness before harm occurs. Critics argue that the compliance costs this imposes may disadvantage smaller institutions and European edtech startups relative to large incumbents, and that the high-risk classification is broad enough to create uncertainty about which adaptive learning tools or automated feedback systems fall within its scope. These tensions remain live as the Act's provisions phase in through 2026 and 2027.

Outside Europe, the regulatory landscape is considerably more fragmented. The United States has not enacted federal AI legislation, and the governance of AI in education consequently operates through a combination of existing statutes, executive guidance, and state-level action. The Family Educational Rights and Privacy Act (FERPA) and the Children's Online Privacy Protection Act (COPPA) impose constraints on how student data may be collected and shared, providing an indirect check on some AI applications, but neither was designed with algorithmic systems in mind and both leave substantial gaps. The Biden administration's 2023 Executive Order on AI directed federal agencies, including the Department of Education, to develop guidance on AI use, and the Department subsequently published a report on AI in education emphasising transparency and the protection of civil rights. However, executive guidance carries no binding legal force and is subject to revision by successive administrations, a vulnerability that became apparent when the regulatory posture of federal agencies shifted in 2025. Several US states — including California, New York, and Colorado — have moved to fill the vacuum with their own AI legislation, but the resulting variation creates compliance complexity for edtech companies operating nationally.

China presents a third model. Chinese authorities have issued a series of targeted regulations addressing specific AI applications, including rules on algorithmic recommendation systems (2022) and generative AI services (2023), both of which apply to educational platforms. The generative AI regulations require providers to label AI-generated content, prevent the production of content that undermines state authority, and conduct security assessments before public release. Enforcement is centralised and relatively swift by international standards. The philosophical orientation differs fundamentally from the EU's: where European regulation foregrounds individual rights and procedural fairness, Chinese regulation emphasises social stability, content control, and alignment with national development objectives. Whether this produces better or worse outcomes for learners is a genuinely contested question that available evidence does not yet resolve.

Other significant jurisdictions occupy intermediate positions. The United Kingdom, following its departure from the EU, has opted for a principles-based, sector-led approach coordinated through existing regulators rather than a single AI statute, with the Office for Qualifications and Examinations Regulation and the Information Commissioner's Office each addressing aspects of AI in education within their existing mandates. UNESCO's 2021 Recommendation on the Ethics of AI, while non-binding, has shaped national policy conversations in dozens of member states and introduced a common vocabulary around human oversight, transparency, and non-discrimination.

What emerges from this comparative picture is a fundamental divergence not merely in regulatory technique but in underlying values. The EU bets on ex ante legal requirements to prevent harm; the United States has historically preferred market mechanisms and litigation; China uses regulation primarily as an instrument of governance rather than individual protection. For educational institutions operating across borders — and for the students whose opportunities are shaped by algorithmic systems — the absence of international harmonisation is itself a significant regulatory fact.

Sources (edition 72, 6 sources)

What guidance have major international bodies (UNESCO, OECD, EU, and others) issued on AI in education, and how is it being implemented?

## International Guidance on Artificial Intelligence in Education

The arrival of generative AI in classrooms and research institutions has prompted an unusually swift response from international bodies, each approaching the challenge through its own institutional lens while converging on a set of shared concerns: learner protection, equity, teacher agency, and the need for governance frameworks that can keep pace with rapidly evolving technology.

UNESCO has been the most prolific producer of normative guidance in this space. Its engagement stretches back to the 2019 Beijing Consensus on Artificial Intelligence and Education, which established early principles around human-centred design, inclusion, and the primacy of educational values over technological imperatives. That foundation was substantially built upon when UNESCO released its Guidance for Generative AI in Education and Research in September 2023, the product of a global consultation process that gave the document unusual breadth of input. The guidance explicitly calls on governments to regulate generative AI tools before deploying them in educational settings, a notably cautious posture that reflects UNESCO's longstanding concern with protecting vulnerable learners. Central to the document is the argument that no generative AI application should enter a school or university without prior assessment of its pedagogical value, its data practices, and its potential to exacerbate existing inequalities. UNESCO also insists that teachers must remain the authoritative figures in learning relationships, with AI positioned as a supplementary instrument rather than a substitute for human judgement. The organisation has paired this normative work with practical tools, including a competency framework for educators and a recommendation that member states designate minimum ages for student interaction with generative AI systems — a provision that has attracted both support and criticism, with some researchers arguing that blanket age thresholds are blunt instruments poorly suited to the diversity of educational contexts across 193 member states.

The OECD has approached the question from a somewhat different angle, emphasising skills, labour market readiness, and the measurement of learning outcomes. Its AI in Education work sits within a broader programme on digital education and is closely connected to the OECD's Principles on AI, adopted in 2019 and subsequently endorsed by G20 nations, which stress transparency, accountability, and robustness. In the education context, the OECD has focused particularly on what it terms "AI literacy" — the capacity of students and teachers to understand, critically evaluate, and work alongside AI systems. Its analyses of national education systems suggest that readiness varies enormously, with high-income countries generally better positioned to integrate AI tools responsibly, while lower-income systems risk being left to adopt technologies designed elsewhere, with little capacity to audit or adapt them. The OECD has stopped short of prescriptive regulation, preferring to offer comparative data and policy options, which gives its guidance a more advisory than directive character.

The European Union has moved furthest toward binding governance. The EU AI Act, which reached political agreement in late 2023 and entered into force in 2024, classifies certain AI applications in education as high-risk — specifically those used for student assessment, admissions decisions, or monitoring of learner behaviour. High-risk classification triggers requirements for transparency, human oversight, data governance, and conformity assessments before deployment. This represents a qualitatively different kind of guidance from UNESCO or OECD recommendations: it carries legal force within member states and creates compliance obligations for developers and institutions alike. The European Commission has also issued supplementary guidance through its Digital Education Action Plan, which encourages member states to develop national AI in education strategies and invest in teacher training. Implementation, however, remains uneven across the bloc, with northern European countries generally further advanced in both policy development and classroom integration than southern and eastern members.

Beyond these three bodies, the G7 and G20 have issued communiqués endorsing responsible AI principles in education, though these tend to restate existing frameworks rather than break new ground. The Council of Europe has contributed work on AI and human rights that bears on educational contexts, particularly regarding surveillance and the rights of minors.

The central tension running through all of this guidance is the gap between normative ambition and practical implementation. UNESCO's recommendations are non-binding, and member states vary enormously in their capacity and willingness to act on them. The OECD's influence operates through soft power and peer comparison rather than enforcement. Even the EU AI Act, the most legally robust instrument, faces questions about how educational institutions — many of them under-resourced — will manage compliance in practice. Researchers have noted that guidance produced at the international level often reflects the priorities and technological contexts of wealthy nations, and that its translation into diverse local settings requires adaptation that international bodies are not always well placed to support. Whether the current wave of frameworks will produce durable change in how AI is used in classrooms, or whether it will remain largely aspirational, is a question that the evidence does not yet resolve.

What does an effective institutional AI policy contain, and what distinguishes policies that work from those ignored in practice?

# What Effective Institutional AI Policy Contains, and Why Most Policies Fail in Practice

When generative AI tools became widely accessible in late 2022 and throughout 2023, educational institutions faced immediate pressure to respond. The result, across higher education in particular, was a wave of hastily drafted policies that satisfied the demand for institutional action without necessarily guiding it. The EDUCAUSE 2024 AI Landscape Study, which examined dozens of institutional policy documents, found that the majority fell into one of two failure modes: they were either so vague as to offer no practical direction, or so rigid that they became obsolete within months as the tools themselves evolved. Understanding what separates effective policy from performative policy requires examining both the structural features of the documents themselves and the institutional conditions that determine whether any policy is actually followed.

The most durable policies, according to the EDUCAUSE analysis, avoid the trap of naming specific tools and instead specify categories of acceptable and unacceptable use. A policy that prohibits "ChatGPT" by name is already partially outdated; a policy that addresses the use of AI-generated text submitted without disclosure, or the feeding of confidential student data into external language models, remains relevant regardless of which particular system is involved. This categorical approach requires more careful drafting, because it demands that institutions think through the underlying principles — around academic integrity, data privacy, equity of access, and pedagogical purpose — rather than reacting to a specific product. The effort, however, pays dividends in longevity and applicability.

Specificity and adaptability are not opposites, but achieving both simultaneously is genuinely difficult. Effective policies tend to resolve this tension by operating at two levels: a stable core of principles and prohibited conduct that changes rarely, and a set of implementation guidelines or annexes that can be updated more frequently as circumstances shift. The core might establish that undisclosed AI assistance in assessed work constitutes a breach of academic integrity, while the implementation guidance addresses how disclosure should be made, what counts as meaningful human authorship, and how instructors should handle ambiguous cases. Separating these layers allows the institution to respond to new developments without reopening foundational debates every time a new tool appears.

What the evidence consistently shows is that the content of a policy, however well-crafted, is a necessary but insufficient condition for compliance. The more consequential variable is the process by which the policy was developed. Policies that emerge from genuine consultation — involving faculty, students, academic integrity officers, legal counsel, and IT — tend to be followed more consistently than those handed down from senior administration or legal teams alone. This is partly because consultation surfaces practical objections and edge cases that top-down drafting misses, and partly because stakeholders who participated in shaping a policy feel a degree of ownership over it. A faculty member who argued for a particular formulation during a working group is more likely to apply it thoughtfully in their course than one who received the final document by email.

Training and communication matter enormously, and here most institutions have underinvested. A policy document, however clear, does not automatically translate into changed behaviour. Faculty need concrete guidance on how to apply categorical rules to specific situations — whether a student using AI to generate a first draft and then substantially revising it constitutes a violation, for instance, is a question that reasonable people answer differently, and policy silence on such cases produces inconsistency. Students, meanwhile, frequently report confusion about what is and is not permitted, particularly when policies vary across courses or departments. The EDUCAUSE findings suggest that institutions with lower rates of policy confusion tend to be those that have invested in worked examples, scenario-based training, and accessible points of contact for questions.

There is also a structural tension that no amount of good drafting fully resolves: the gap between institutional policy and course-level practice. Many instructors treat AI policy as a matter of individual pedagogical discretion, adjusting permissions assignment by assignment. This is not inherently unreasonable — the appropriate role of AI assistance in a creative writing workshop differs from its role in a statistics exam — but it creates a fragmented landscape that students find difficult to navigate. Effective institutional policy tends to acknowledge this reality explicitly, establishing a baseline of institutional expectations while granting instructors defined latitude to be more permissive or more restrictive within their courses, provided they communicate their position clearly in course materials.

What distinguishes policies that work, then, is not any single feature but a combination: categorical rather than tool-specific framing, a layered structure that separates stable principles from updatable guidance, genuine stakeholder involvement in drafting, investment in training and communication, and explicit acknowledgment of the course-level variation that will inevitably occur. Institutions that treat policy as a compliance exercise — something to publish and file — will find it ignored. Those that treat it as the beginning of an ongoing institutional conversation about the role of AI in learning are more likely to find it shaping practice in the ways they intend.

How are education systems regulating student data privacy in AI tools, and which legal frameworks apply to minors?

## Regulating Student Data Privacy in the Age of AI Educational Tools

The rapid adoption of artificial intelligence in classrooms has exposed a fundamental mismatch between the pace of technological change and the legal frameworks designed to protect students. Across jurisdictions, regulators are attempting to apply laws conceived in an earlier era to tools that collect, infer, and act upon student data in ways their drafters could not have anticipated. The result is a patchwork of protections that varies considerably by country, age group, and institutional context — and that leaves significant gaps precisely where the risks are greatest.

In the United States, the Family Educational Rights and Privacy Act (FERPA) remains the primary federal instrument governing student data privacy. Enacted in 1974, FERPA was built around a straightforward premise: educational institutions that receive federal funding must protect the confidentiality of students' education records, and must grant parents — or students themselves once they turn eighteen — the right to inspect, correct, and control the disclosure of those records. For decades, this framework functioned adequately in a world of paper transcripts and administrative databases. Its application to AI-driven educational tools is considerably more strained.

The central difficulty is definitional. FERPA's protections attach to "education records," a category that courts and regulators have interpreted to mean documents directly related to a student and maintained by an institution or its agents. Many AI tools, however, generate data that does not fit neatly into this category. When an adaptive learning platform infers a student's cognitive state from keystroke patterns, or when a large language model retains conversational data to personalise future interactions, the resulting information may not constitute an education record in the traditional sense — even though it is arguably more sensitive than a grade report. Whether such inferred or behaviorally derived data falls under FERPA's umbrella remains a genuinely contested question, and federal guidance has not resolved it definitively.

FERPA also relies heavily on the concept of institutional control. Schools are responsible for ensuring that third-party vendors who access student data under a "school official" exception comply with the law's requirements. In practice, enforcing this obligation across a proliferating ecosystem of AI vendors — many of which operate across multiple jurisdictions and update their data practices frequently — has proven difficult. The U.S. Department of Education has issued guidance encouraging institutions to conduct vendor due diligence, but the enforcement mechanism remains weak: FERPA's primary sanction is the withdrawal of federal funding, a remedy so drastic that it is almost never applied.

Supplementing FERPA for younger students is the Children's Online Privacy Protection Act (COPPA), which imposes stricter requirements on operators of websites and online services directed at children under thirteen, including verifiable parental consent before collecting personal information. COPPA is more directly applicable to many AI tools than FERPA, but it too was designed before generative AI existed, and its consent mechanisms sit awkwardly with school-mediated deployments where parents may have little visibility into which tools their children are using on any given day.

At the state level, the regulatory landscape has grown considerably more active. California's Student Online Personal Information Protection Act (SOPIPA) and its successors prohibit ed-tech operators from using student data for targeted advertising or selling it to third parties — obligations that go beyond what FERPA requires. Several other states have enacted similar legislation, and a small number have begun explicitly addressing AI. These state-level efforts represent genuine progress, but their fragmentation creates compliance complexity for vendors and leaves students in less legislatively active states with weaker protections.

Outside the United States, the European Union's General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) provides a more comprehensive baseline. GDPR treats children's data as a special category requiring heightened protection, mandates data minimisation and purpose limitation, and grants individuals robust rights of access, rectification, and erasure. Member states may set the age of digital consent anywhere between thirteen and sixteen, creating some variation within the bloc, but the underlying framework is substantially more demanding than its American counterparts. The EU's AI Act, which entered into force in 2024, adds a further layer by classifying certain AI applications in education — particularly those used for assessment or monitoring — as high-risk systems subject to mandatory transparency, human oversight, and conformity assessments before deployment.

What emerges from this comparative picture is a clear tension between the comprehensiveness of rights-based frameworks like GDPR and the more fragmented, institution-centred approach that characterises U.S. law. Neither model has yet fully grappled with the distinctive features of modern AI: its capacity to generate sensitive inferences from innocuous inputs, its opacity, and the difficulty of obtaining meaningful consent when the data subject is a child and the technology is embedded in a compulsory educational setting. The regulatory conversation is genuinely open, and the adequacy of existing frameworks for protecting minors in AI-mediated education remains one of the more pressing unresolved questions in education policy.

How should institutions handle procurement of AI tools — evaluation criteria, vendor accountability, contract terms, and exit strategies?

## Procuring AI in Education: Evaluation, Accountability, and the Art of the Exit

When educational institutions consider adopting AI tools, the temptation is to treat procurement as a technical exercise — comparing feature lists, sitting through vendor demonstrations, and negotiating price. The evidence suggests this approach is inadequate and, in some cases, actively harmful. Institutions that have fared best in AI adoption, according to the EDUCAUSE 2023 AI Landscape Study, approached procurement as a pedagogical and governance challenge first, and a technical one second. That reordering of priorities has significant consequences for how evaluation criteria are constructed, how vendors are held accountable, and how contracts are written to protect institutional interests over time.

Evaluation as a Multi-Dimensional Exercise

The most durable lesson from recent institutional experience is that technical performance and pedagogical validity are not the same thing, and conflating them is a reliable path to disappointment. A tool may perform impressively on benchmark tasks while failing to support the specific learning outcomes an institution cares about, introducing accessibility barriers for students with disabilities, or encoding demographic biases that disadvantage already-marginalised groups. Institutions that developed multi-dimensional evaluation rubrics — assessing pedagogical alignment, accessibility compliance, data governance practices, and equity impact before any pilot with students — consistently reported smoother adoption and fewer downstream crises.

This means evaluation committees should include not only IT and procurement staff but instructional designers, faculty, disability services officers, and ideally student representatives. Equity impact assessments deserve particular emphasis: AI systems trained on unrepresentative data can systematically underserve the students who most need support, and this risk is rarely visible in a vendor's promotional materials. Accessibility compliance, governed in many jurisdictions by law as well as institutional policy, must be verified independently rather than accepted on a vendor's assurance.

Vendor Accountability: Beyond the Sales Relationship

Once a tool clears initial evaluation, the question becomes how to maintain meaningful accountability throughout the relationship. Vendors of AI products occupy an unusual position: their systems can change substantially through model updates, retraining, or shifts in underlying infrastructure, sometimes without explicit notification to institutional clients. A system that passed an equity audit in January may behave differently by September. Contracts should therefore require vendors to notify institutions of material changes to model behaviour, training data, or algorithmic logic, and to provide updated documentation sufficient for the institution to reassess compliance.

Transparency obligations extend to explainability. Where AI tools inform consequential decisions — flagging students as at-risk, generating feedback on assessed work, or influencing course recommendations — institutions have both ethical and, increasingly, legal reasons to understand how those outputs are produced. Vendors who cannot or will not provide meaningful explanations of their systems' reasoning should be treated with considerable caution. This is an area where the field remains contested: full algorithmic transparency is technically difficult and commercially sensitive, and reasonable people disagree about how much explainability is achievable or necessary. What is not contested is that institutions should ask the question explicitly and document the answer before signing.

Contract Terms That Protect Institutional Interests

The contract is where good intentions either become enforceable commitments or evaporate. Several provisions deserve particular attention. Data ownership and data use clauses must be unambiguous: student data generated through use of an AI tool should not be available to the vendor for model retraining or commercial purposes without explicit, informed consent. Given the sensitivity of educational records and the legal protections that govern them in most jurisdictions, this is non-negotiable rather than aspirational.

Service-level agreements should specify not only uptime but performance standards relevant to educational use — response accuracy, bias metrics, and accessibility conformance — with defined consequences for failure. Audit rights, allowing the institution or a designated third party to inspect the system's behaviour periodically, provide a mechanism for ongoing accountability rather than a one-time evaluation. Where institutions lack the internal expertise to conduct such audits, consortium arrangements with peer institutions or engagement of specialist third parties are increasingly common and worth the investment.

Exit Strategies: Planning for the End from the Beginning

Perhaps the most neglected dimension of AI procurement is the exit strategy. Vendor lock-in is a structural risk in AI adoption: proprietary data formats, deep integration with institutional systems, and the practical difficulty of migrating accumulated student data can make it prohibitively expensive to leave a vendor even when the relationship has deteriorated. Contracts should specify data portability rights in open, documented formats, define the conditions and timelines under which the institution can terminate without penalty, and require the vendor to support a transition period during which data can be extracted and migrated.

The broader principle underlying all of these considerations is that AI procurement in education is not a transaction but a relationship with significant power asymmetries — and that the institution's leverage is greatest before the contract is signed. Investing in rigorous evaluation, demanding enforceable accountability, and planning for exit from the outset are not bureaucratic cautions but the practical conditions for responsible adoption.

How are examination and credentialing bodies redesigning high-stakes assessment for the AI era?

# High-Stakes Assessment in the Age of Artificial Intelligence

The arrival of capable generative AI systems has forced examination and credentialing bodies into a reckoning that many had deferred for decades. When a large language model can produce a competent essay, solve structured problems, and navigate multiple-choice formats with accuracy that rivals or exceeds the average candidate, the validity of traditional written examinations as measures of individual knowledge becomes genuinely difficult to defend. The response from major testing organisations has been neither uniform nor fully resolved, but a discernible set of strategic directions has emerged.

The most significant structural shift is the move toward assessment formats that resist AI delegation — not by detecting AI use after the fact, but by designing tasks that require real-time human presence and reasoning. The International Baccalaureate announced in 2023 that it was conducting a comprehensive review of its entire assessment portfolio, with particular attention to components that could be completed outside supervised conditions. This reflects a broader recognition among credentialing bodies that the traditional take-home essay and unsupervised coursework component, long valued for allowing extended reflection, have become epistemically compromised. When the process of composition can be outsourced invisibly, the product can no longer reliably attest to the producer's understanding.

Oral examinations and live performance tasks have consequently attracted renewed institutional interest. These formats are not new — viva voce examinations have existed in European universities for centuries — but they had been progressively marginalised in mass credentialing systems on grounds of cost, scalability, and concerns about inter-rater reliability. AI has reversed that calculus, at least partially. A candidate who cannot explain, defend, or extend their written work under direct questioning is exposed in ways that no detection algorithm can achieve. Several professional credentialing bodies in law, medicine, and accountancy have moved to expand the proportion of assessment conducted under direct observation, whether in person or via proctored video examination.

Yet the shift is not without genuine tensions. Oral and performance-based assessments introduce their own validity threats, particularly around equity. Research on viva examinations consistently shows that candidates from certain linguistic, cultural, and socioeconomic backgrounds perform differently under oral conditions than their written work would predict — and not always in ways that reflect underlying competence rather than familiarity with the register and conventions of academic discourse. Scaling oral assessment to the hundreds of thousands of candidates who sit major school-leaving or professional examinations each year also raises serious logistical and financial questions that remain unresolved. The equity concern is not merely theoretical: if AI-resistant formats systematically disadvantage already-marginalised groups, the cure may introduce new distortions even as it addresses old ones.

A parallel and somewhat different approach involves redesigning written assessments to require the kind of situated, contextual, and process-visible reasoning that AI cannot straightforwardly supply. This includes examinations that ask candidates to analyse novel, locally specific, or deliberately incomplete information — material that would not appear in any training corpus — and to show their reasoning in stages rather than simply producing a final answer. Some bodies have experimented with requiring candidates to annotate their own drafts, respond to examiner interventions mid-task, or complete assessments in two stages, with the second stage conducted under supervision and explicitly building on the first. These designs attempt to preserve the cognitive richness of extended writing tasks while reintroducing the kind of accountability that supervised conditions provide.

There is also a contested debate about whether AI should be excluded from high-stakes assessment at all, or whether the more honest response is to redesign what is being assessed. Proponents of AI-integrated assessment argue that if professional practice in law, medicine, engineering, and virtually every other field will involve working alongside AI tools, then assessing candidates in conditions that prohibit those tools produces a credential of diminishing real-world validity. On this view, the examination of the future should assess how well a candidate can direct, evaluate, and critically interrogate AI-generated outputs — skills that are genuinely difficult to fake and genuinely important to cultivate. This position remains a minority one among established credentialing bodies, most of which have opted for restriction rather than integration, but it is gaining traction in some professional and higher education contexts.

What is clear is that no single solution has achieved consensus, and the field is in a period of active, sometimes anxious, experimentation. Detection-based approaches — software designed to identify AI-generated text — have proven unreliable, with documented rates of both false positives and false negatives that make them unsuitable as the primary line of defence in high-stakes contexts. The more durable responses are structural: changing what is assessed, how it is observed, and what counts as evidence of genuine understanding. The examination systems that emerge from this period will likely look meaningfully different from those that preceded it, though the shape of that difference remains, for now, genuinely open.

What academic-integrity frameworks have institutions adopted to replace binary "cheating/not cheating" models, and how are they working?

## Beyond Cheating and Not Cheating: How Institutions Are Rethinking Academic Integrity

For most of the twentieth century, academic integrity operated on a simple binary: a student either cheated or did not, and the institutional response followed accordingly. That model, however clean in theory, proved increasingly inadequate as the realities of student work grew more complex — collaborative writing cultures, patchwork paraphrasing, contract cheating services, and, most recently, generative artificial intelligence have all exposed the binary's inability to capture what actually happens when students produce academic work. In response, a significant number of institutions have moved toward what researchers associated with the International Center for Academic Integrity (ICAI) describe as "graduated" or "spectrum-based" frameworks, which treat academic dishonesty not as a single category but as a continuum of severity and intentionality.

The conceptual shift is substantial. Where the old model asked only whether a violation occurred, spectrum-based frameworks ask a series of more granular questions: How serious was the breach? Was it deliberate or the product of misunderstanding? Was the student a first offender or a repeat one? Did the conduct involve personal gain at the expense of others, or did it reflect confusion about norms? These distinctions matter because they allow institutions to calibrate responses — educational interventions for minor or inadvertent infractions, formal sanctions for deliberate or serious ones — rather than applying the same disciplinary machinery to a student who miscited a source and one who purchased an essay wholesale.

The practical architecture of these frameworks varies, but several common features have emerged. Many institutions now operate tiered systems in which low-level infractions, such as poor paraphrasing or incomplete attribution, are handled at the course level through mandatory academic-skills instruction rather than formal adjudication. Mid-level violations — deliberate plagiarism, unauthorized collaboration — typically trigger departmental review with outcomes ranging from grade penalties to required integrity modules. The most serious offences, including contract cheating and systematic deception, are escalated to institutional panels with the capacity to impose suspension or expulsion. Crucially, most of these systems build in a record-keeping mechanism that allows a pattern of lower-level infractions to be recognized and treated more seriously over time, addressing the concern that lenient handling of early violations enables escalation.

The evidence on how well these frameworks are working is genuinely mixed, and intellectual honesty requires acknowledging that tension. On the positive side, institutions that have adopted spectrum models report higher rates of faculty reporting, a finding that makes intuitive sense: instructors who previously avoided formal processes because they seemed disproportionate to minor infractions are more willing to engage when a proportionate, educational response is available. There is also some evidence that students who receive educational rather than purely punitive responses to first offences show lower rates of subsequent violations, suggesting that the rehabilitative logic underlying these frameworks has some empirical support.

At the same time, critics raise legitimate concerns. One persistent problem is inconsistency: when discretion is distributed across individual instructors and departments, students in different programs or with different instructors may receive dramatically different treatment for comparable conduct, raising equity concerns. This is not a trivial objection. Research on how bias operates in academic misconduct proceedings suggests that students from underrepresented groups are sometimes judged more harshly, and a system that multiplies the points at which individual judgment is exercised may amplify rather than reduce that disparity. Institutions have attempted to address this through calibration training and standardized rubrics, but whether those interventions are sufficient remains an open question.

The arrival of generative AI has stress-tested these frameworks in ways that were not anticipated when most of them were designed. AI-assisted writing does not map neatly onto existing categories: it is not plagiarism in the traditional sense, it may or may not involve deliberate deception depending on whether the student understood the rules, and the degree of reliance on AI output varies enormously. Some institutions have responded by adding AI-specific tiers to their existing frameworks, distinguishing between undisclosed AI use, partial AI assistance with human revision, and wholesale AI generation. Others have opted for a more radical rethink, treating AI as a tool whose permissibility is assignment-specific rather than categorically prohibited or permitted. Neither approach has yet accumulated enough longitudinal data to permit confident evaluation.

What the evidence does support, with reasonable confidence, is that the binary model it replaced was not working. High rates of unreported violations, faculty reluctance to engage formal processes, and student confusion about norms all pointed to a system that was simultaneously too blunt and too punitive to function well. The spectrum-based frameworks now being adopted represent a more sophisticated and arguably more honest account of how academic misconduct actually occurs. Whether they will prove durable as AI continues to reshape what student work looks like is the central unresolved question facing the field.

  • https://academicintegrity.org/resources/fundamental-values (practitioner)
  • https://journalhosting.ucalgary.ca/index.php/cjeap/article/view/75415 (practitioner)

How are teacher unions, professional bodies, and accreditation agencies responding to AI, and what standards are they setting?

# Institutional Responses to AI in Education: Unions, Professional Bodies, and Accreditation Agencies

The arrival of generative AI in classrooms has prompted a wave of institutional responses from the organizations that govern, represent, and credential the teaching profession. These responses vary considerably in tone and ambition — from cautious labor-protective frameworks to emerging competency standards — but they share a common recognition that AI's integration into education cannot be left to individual schools or technology vendors alone. Taken together, they represent the early architecture of a professional consensus that is still very much under construction.

The most prominent labor response in the United States has come from the National Education Association, which with approximately three million members is the country's largest teacher union. In 2023, the NEA adopted a formal policy statement that places educator agency at the center of any legitimate AI deployment. The union's position is unambiguous on several points: AI tools must not be used to surveil, evaluate, or replace teachers, and any introduction of such systems into schools must involve meaningful consultation with educators rather than being imposed unilaterally by administrators or district technology offices. The NEA frames AI primarily as a labor question before it is a pedagogical one, insisting that transparency about how these tools function and what data they collect is a precondition for their acceptable use. This worker-centered framework reflects a broader anxiety within organized labor that AI could be used to intensify monitoring of employees or to justify workforce reductions — concerns that are not unique to education but take on particular weight in a profession already navigating chronic underfunding and high attrition.

What the NEA's position does not yet provide in full is detailed pedagogical guidance: it establishes protective boundaries more confidently than it maps out what responsible, educationally beneficial AI use should look like in practice. This gap is significant, and it points to a tension running through many institutional responses — the difference between protecting educators from AI's risks and equipping them to use it well.

Professional bodies focused on teacher preparation and development have begun to address that gap, though the work remains at an early stage. Organizations concerned with educator competency frameworks have started to identify AI literacy as a dimension of professional practice, arguing that teachers need not only to understand how AI tools work in general terms but to exercise critical judgment about when and whether to deploy them with students. The emerging view is that AI competency for educators is not simply a technical skill but a professional and ethical one — encompassing questions of bias, data privacy, equity of access, and the integrity of student assessment. Whether these competency frameworks will be formally embedded in teacher licensure requirements remains an open and contested question; the institutional will to do so varies considerably across jurisdictions.

Accreditation agencies, which hold significant leverage over teacher preparation programs through their approval authority, have been slower to move but are beginning to signal that AI readiness will become a criterion for program evaluation. The logic is straightforward: if graduating teachers are expected to navigate AI-rich classrooms, then preparation programs must demonstrate that they are training candidates accordingly. What "accordingly" means in practice — whether it requires dedicated coursework, integrated field experience, or demonstrated competency assessments — has not yet been standardized, and different accrediting bodies are at different points in developing their expectations.

Internationally, the picture is similarly uneven. Some national teacher associations and ministries of education have moved more quickly than others to issue guidance, with UNESCO's 2023 framework on generative AI in education providing a reference point that several professional bodies have drawn upon. That framework emphasizes human oversight, the protection of student data, and the importance of teacher judgment remaining central to educational decisions — themes that echo the NEA's domestic position and suggest a degree of convergence in principle, even where implementation diverges.

The honest assessment of where things stand is that institutional responses to AI in education are real but incomplete. The protective instincts of teacher unions are well-founded and have produced meaningful policy commitments around transparency and labor rights. Professional bodies are beginning to articulate what AI competency should mean for educators, but the translation of those articulations into formal standards and licensure requirements is still in progress. Accreditation agencies are moving toward incorporating AI readiness into their evaluative criteria, but the specifics remain underdeveloped. The field is, in short, in a period of standard-setting rather than settled standards — with the major institutional actors having staked out positions and begun the slower work of turning those positions into durable professional infrastructure.

What funding models and public investments in AI for education exist, and what do early returns suggest about their effectiveness?

## Funding Models and Public Investments in AI for Education

The question of how governments and institutions fund artificial intelligence in education—and whether those investments are paying off—sits at an awkward intersection of policy ambition and evidentiary caution. What the available evidence reveals is a landscape of significant rhetorical commitment, growing but unevenly distributed public expenditure, and early returns that are promising in narrow contexts yet far too thin to support sweeping conclusions.

In the United States, the primary federal vehicle has been the National AI Initiative Act of 2020, which authorized coordinated research and development across federal agencies, including the Department of Education. That legislation established a whole-of-government posture toward AI rather than a dedicated education funding stream, meaning that education-specific investments have largely flowed through existing grant mechanisms—the Institute of Education Sciences, the National Science Foundation's education directorates, and competitive grant programs under the Every Student Succeeds Act—rather than through any purpose-built AI-in-education fund. The Department of Education's 2023 report, Artificial Intelligence and the Future of Teaching and Learning, articulated a policy framework emphasizing equity, transparency, and teacher agency, but it stopped short of announcing new appropriations. The result is a federal posture that is strategically coherent but fiscally modest relative to the scale of the ambition it describes.

This gap between framework and funding is not unique to the United States. Across OECD nations, AI education initiatives have tended to emerge from broader digital transformation budgets rather than from ring-fenced education-AI allocations. The European Union's investments through Horizon Europe and the Digital Education Action Plan have directed funds toward research consortia and pilot programs, but the translation from pilot to system-wide deployment has been slow, and independent evaluations of outcomes remain sparse. China represents a partial exception: provincial and national governments there have made substantial, centrally coordinated investments in AI-driven adaptive learning platforms, particularly in mathematics and language instruction, and some of those deployments operate at a scale—tens of millions of students—that generates meaningful outcome data. Early results from Chinese implementations suggest measurable gains in test performance under controlled conditions, though researchers have raised legitimate questions about whether those gains reflect genuine learning or optimisation toward narrow assessment targets.

The philanthropic and venture capital sectors have filled some of the gap left by cautious public funders. Organisations such as the Gates Foundation and Chan Zuckerberg Initiative have invested heavily in personalised learning platforms with AI components, and the edtech sector attracted record venture investment in the years surrounding the COVID-19 pandemic. The difficulty with privately funded models is that they generate proprietary data and face weak incentives for rigorous independent evaluation. A company whose product shows null results in a randomised controlled trial has little reason to publish those results, which creates a publication landscape skewed toward positive findings. This selection effect makes it genuinely difficult to assess the aggregate effectiveness of AI tutoring systems, automated feedback tools, and adaptive curricula from the existing literature alone.

Where rigorous evidence does exist, it is cautiously encouraging in specific domains. Intelligent tutoring systems with long development histories—Carnegie Learning's MATHia platform being the most studied example—have demonstrated statistically significant learning gains in algebra compared to traditional instruction, with effect sizes in the range of 0.2 to 0.4 standard deviations in well-controlled studies. Automated writing feedback tools have shown similar modest but real benefits for revision quality in secondary and post-secondary settings. These are not trivial effects, but they are also not transformative, and they come from systems that have been refined over decades rather than from the newer generation of large language model-based tools, for which outcome data is almost entirely absent.

The equity dimension of public investment deserves particular attention. There is a well-documented pattern in which AI educational tools are adopted first by well-resourced schools and districts, meaning that public subsidies—when they do exist—often flow toward institutions that would have adopted technology regardless. Targeted funding programs that direct AI resources specifically toward under-resourced schools exist in several U.S. states and in some European national programs, but their scale is limited and their effectiveness has not been systematically evaluated.

What the evidence suggests, taken together, is that the funding architecture for AI in education is fragmented, under-evaluated, and unevenly distributed. The most defensible conclusion is that targeted, well-designed AI tools can produce meaningful learning gains in specific subjects and contexts, but that the public investment required to realise those gains at scale—including not just technology procurement but teacher training, infrastructure, and independent evaluation—has not yet materialised in most jurisdictions. The gap between the sophistication of the policy rhetoric and the modesty of the evidentiary base remains, for now, the defining feature of this field.

Chapter 10. Chapter 10: Practical Navigation — Guides for Teachers, Students, and Leaders

How should a teacher decide, lesson by lesson, when AI use helps learning and when it undermines it — is there a practical decision framework?

## When to Use AI in the Classroom: A Practical Framework for Teachers

The arrival of capable AI tools in education has forced a question that curriculum theory has always circled but never had to answer quite so urgently: when does assistance become substitution, and when does substitution become harm? The answer cannot be given once and applied universally. It must be worked out lesson by lesson, objective by objective, and the research emerging from cognitive psychology and educational practice offers a principled basis for doing so.

The most durable starting point comes from work on desirable difficulties, a concept developed by Robert Bjork and colleagues over several decades. The core finding is well established: learning is not the same as performance. Conditions that make practice feel easier — removing obstacles, providing immediate answers, smoothing over confusion — tend to improve short-term performance while degrading long-term retention and the ability to transfer knowledge to new contexts. Struggle, when it is productive rather than merely frustrating, is not an obstacle to learning; it is frequently the mechanism of learning. Researchers at the University of Melbourne and elsewhere have applied this directly to AI use, arguing that the central question a teacher must ask before any lesson is whether the AI tool is removing a difficulty that the student needs to encounter, or whether it is removing friction that has no educational value.

This distinction — between productive and unproductive difficulty — is the axis around which any practical framework must turn. Not all struggle is desirable. A student who cannot access a text because of a language barrier, or who is spending cognitive resources on low-level formatting rather than argumentation, is not benefiting from that difficulty. AI assistance in such cases frees working memory for the intellectual work that actually constitutes the learning objective. By contrast, a student who uses an AI to generate the first draft of an essay, or to solve the mathematical problem before attempting it independently, has offloaded precisely the cognitive process the lesson was designed to develop. The output may look identical; the learning is not.

This suggests a first practical test: identify what cognitive process the lesson is trying to build, and ask whether AI use would exercise or bypass that process. Writing a thesis statement, constructing a logical argument, debugging code, interpreting a primary source — these are processes, not products. If the AI performs the process, the student has observed rather than practised. If the AI assists with surrounding tasks (checking grammar after drafting, retrieving background information before analysis, formatting after composition), the core process remains intact.

A second dimension concerns the student's current level of expertise, and here the research introduces a genuine tension. There is reasonable evidence that novice learners and expert learners respond differently to the same kind of assistance. For novices, worked examples and guided support reduce unproductive cognitive overload and allow schemas to form; for more advanced learners, the same guidance can paradoxically impede progress by preventing the effortful retrieval and problem-solving that consolidates expertise. This is sometimes called the expertise reversal effect. Applied to AI, it suggests that a tool which genuinely helps a struggling beginner navigate an unfamiliar domain may be actively counterproductive for a student who has sufficient foundation to work independently. The same AI, the same lesson, the same task — but a different verdict depending on where the student sits in their learning trajectory. Teachers must therefore calibrate not just to the task but to the learner, which is demanding but not unprecedented; it is what good differentiation has always required.

A third consideration is metacognitive: does the student know what the AI is doing and why? There is emerging, though not yet conclusive, evidence that students who use AI tools without understanding their own learning process are less able to self-regulate, less likely to notice when they have not understood something, and more likely to conflate fluent output with genuine comprehension. This points toward a pedagogical practice of making AI use explicit and reflective — asking students to articulate what they did themselves, what the AI contributed, and what they would do differently without it. Whether this metacognitive scaffolding reliably improves outcomes is still an open question, but the theoretical basis is sound and the practical cost is low.

Taken together, these lines of research converge on a framework with three sequential questions. First: what is the learning process this lesson is designed to develop? Second: does the proposed AI use exercise or bypass that process, given where this student currently is? Third: is the student positioned to reflect on and regulate their own use of the tool? Where the answers are, respectively, "clearly identified," "exercises it," and "yes," AI use is likely to support learning. Where any answer is uncertain or unfavourable, restraint is warranted — not as technophobia, but as fidelity to what learning actually requires.

What are the current best practices for designing AI-resilient and AI-inclusive assignments and assessments?

# Designing for the Age of Generative AI: Assessment Practices That Are Both Resilient and Inclusive

The arrival of capable generative AI tools in higher and secondary education has forced a fundamental reconsideration of what assessments are actually for. When a language model can produce a competent five-paragraph essay in seconds, the traditional take-home writing assignment stops functioning as evidence of student learning and starts functioning as a test of a student's willingness to do it themselves. The response emerging from teaching and learning centers at institutions such as Stanford and MIT is not primarily punitive — it is architectural. The question being asked is not how to catch students using AI, but how to design tasks that remain meaningful whether or not AI is involved.

The most durable answer to that question involves shifting the evaluative weight from final products to the learning process itself. AI tools can generate polished outputs, but they cannot replicate the authentic intellectual journey a particular student takes through a particular problem. Process-oriented assessment captures what AI cannot fake: the evolution of thinking. This means building in drafts with required reflective annotations, asking students to document their decision-making at key stages, or requiring revision memos that explain what changed between versions and why. When the artifact under assessment is the process rather than the product, the student's presence in the work becomes structurally unavoidable. This approach is well-supported by practitioner literature and aligns with longstanding constructivist arguments about learning, though rigorous empirical studies comparing process-oriented and product-oriented assessment outcomes specifically in AI-rich environments remain limited — an important caveat for institutions considering wholesale redesign.

Closely related is the move toward what assessment theorists call "personalization at the task level." Generic prompts — "discuss the causes of World War One," "analyze a poem of your choice" — are maximally vulnerable to AI substitution precisely because they have been answered millions of times in the training data. Assignments that require students to engage with locally specific material, their own prior work, a conversation that happened in class last Tuesday, or a dataset unique to their institution cannot be completed by an AI working alone. The student becomes a necessary collaborator in the production of the response. This is not a trick; it is good pedagogy. Specificity has always produced better thinking than generality, and the AI era has simply made that principle consequential in a new way.

The question of AI-inclusive design sits in productive tension with AI-resilient design, and the field has not yet resolved that tension cleanly. AI-resilient design tries to make AI assistance irrelevant or detectable; AI-inclusive design treats AI as a legitimate tool and assesses students on their capacity to use it critically and effectively. Both orientations have principled defenders. Those who favor inclusion argue that prohibiting AI in educational settings is both unenforceable and counterproductive, since students will encounter these tools throughout their professional lives and need supervised practice in using them well. Assignments in this mode might ask students to use an AI to generate a first draft and then critique it, identify its errors, and substantially revise it — making the AI's limitations the object of study. Those who favor resilience argue that certain foundational cognitive skills, including the ability to construct an argument from scratch or to hold a complex idea in working memory long enough to write about it, are undermined if AI is always available as a scaffold. Both concerns are legitimate, and the honest answer is that neither approach is universally appropriate: the right design depends on what the assignment is actually trying to develop.

Oral examination and real-time performance assessment have attracted renewed interest as a complement to written work, precisely because they are difficult to outsource. A student who submits an AI-generated essay but cannot discuss its claims in a ten-minute conversation has revealed something important. Viva-style defenses, in-class presentations that require spontaneous response to questions, and live problem-solving sessions all create moments of accountability that written submission alone cannot provide. These methods are not new, but their value has been reappraised.

Equity considerations complicate every recommendation here. Process documentation and oral defense can disadvantage students with certain disabilities, those for whom the language of instruction is not their first language, and those with caregiving responsibilities that make iterative submission difficult. AI-inclusive assignments may inadvertently favor students with reliable internet access and the digital literacy to use these tools effectively. Assessment redesign that ignores these asymmetries risks solving one problem by creating another.

What the evidence supports, taken together, is a pluralistic approach: a portfolio of assessment types across a course or program, designed so that no single modality can be gamed, and so that students with different strengths have multiple opportunities to demonstrate genuine understanding. The goal is not to defeat AI but to make authentic learning visible again.

  • https://library.educause.edu/resources/2023/3/2023-educause-horizon-report-teaching-and-learning-edition (academic) [high credibility]
  • https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/03075079.2019.1582015 (academic) [high credibility]

How can teachers use AI effectively for planning, differentiation, feedback, and administration while safeguarding their professional judgment?

## The Teacher and the Machine: Using AI Effectively While Keeping Professional Judgment Intact

The arrival of capable generative AI in schools has prompted a practical question that cuts through the hype: what can these tools actually do for teachers, and where must human judgment remain sovereign? The evidence, though still accumulating, points toward a coherent answer. AI is most valuable when it handles the generative and administrative labour that consumes teacher time without requiring the relational, contextual, and ethical intelligence that only experienced educators possess.

The case for AI as a planning accelerator is among the most empirically grounded claims in this space. Surveys and practitioner studies consistently report that tools such as ChatGPT, MagicSchool AI, and Curipod can reduce lesson preparation time by somewhere between thirty and fifty percent. A 2023 RAND Corporation survey found that roughly a quarter of American teachers had already adopted AI for lesson planning within the first year of mainstream availability — a remarkably rapid uptake by the standards of educational technology. The mechanism is straightforward: AI can generate a first draft of a lesson structure, suggest discussion questions, produce worked examples at varying levels of complexity, or propose analogies for difficult concepts in seconds. What would have taken an hour of searching, adapting, and typing can be compressed into minutes of prompting and editing.

The critical qualification, however, is that speed of generation does not guarantee pedagogical quality. AI systems trained on general text have no reliable knowledge of a particular class's prior learning, the school's curriculum sequence, local cultural context, or the specific misconceptions a teacher observed in last week's assessment. A generated lesson plan is therefore best understood as a capable but uninformed first draft. The teacher's role shifts from author to editor and critic — a shift that demands its own form of expertise. Teachers who lack strong subject knowledge or pedagogical grounding may accept AI output uncritically, which represents a genuine risk. The time savings are real, but they are only educationally beneficial when the teacher applies informed judgment to what the machine produces.

Differentiation — adapting instruction to the varied needs of learners — is an area where AI shows particular promise precisely because it is so labour-intensive when done well. Generating parallel versions of a text at different reading levels, producing additional practice problems for students who need consolidation, or drafting extension tasks for those ready to go further are all tasks that AI handles competently and quickly. The evidence here is largely practitioner-reported rather than rigorously experimental, and caution is warranted: differentiated materials are only useful if they are well-matched to actual student needs, which requires the teacher's knowledge of individual learners. AI can multiply the options available; it cannot select among them wisely without human direction.

On feedback, the picture is more contested. AI tools can generate written comments on student work with impressive fluency, and some studies suggest that students engage positively with AI-generated formative feedback, particularly when it is specific and actionable. The efficiency gains for teachers managing large marking loads are potentially significant. Yet feedback is not merely information transfer — it is a communicative act embedded in a relationship, and research on effective feedback consistently emphasises that students' responses depend heavily on trust, motivation, and their interpretation of the teacher's intent. Whether AI-generated feedback can replicate these relational dimensions remains genuinely open. There is also a risk that teachers, relieved of the cognitive effort of marking, lose the diagnostic insight that close reading of student work provides. Marking is not only a service to students; it is how teachers learn what their students understand.

Administrative tasks — drafting communications to parents, generating report comment banks, summarising meeting notes, producing policy documents — represent perhaps the least contested application of AI in teaching. These tasks are time-consuming, formulaic, and carry relatively low stakes in terms of professional judgment. The evidence that AI can handle them competently is strong, and the argument for using it here is correspondingly straightforward. Time recovered from administration is time available for the work that requires human presence: conversation, observation, relationship-building, and the responsive improvisation that characterises skilled teaching.

The overarching principle that emerges from the evidence is one of deliberate delegation. AI is a capable generator of drafts, options, and administrative text; it is not a substitute for the teacher's knowledge of context, their ethical responsibility toward students, or their capacity to read a room. The professional risk is not that AI will replace teachers — the evidence does not support that conclusion — but that teachers under time pressure may over-delegate, accepting AI output without the critical scrutiny it requires. Safeguarding professional judgment, then, is less a matter of limiting AI use than of cultivating the habits of mind — subject knowledge, pedagogical awareness, and reflective practice — that make critical engagement with AI output possible in the first place.
