What benchmarks and audits exist for evaluating the pedagogical quality, safety, and bias of educational AI models themselves?

## Measuring What Matters: Benchmarks, Audits, and the Evaluation of Educational AI

The rapid deployment of AI systems in educational settings has outpaced the development of rigorous frameworks for evaluating them. What tools exist tend to measure what is easiest to quantify — factual knowledge, surface-level accuracy, demographic parity in outputs — rather than the qualities that most determine whether an AI system genuinely serves learners. The result is a landscape of evaluation infrastructure that is technically sophisticated in some respects and strikingly underdeveloped in others.

The most widely cited benchmark for assessing AI knowledge in academic domains is the Massive Multitask Language Understanding benchmark, developed by Hendrycks and colleagues at UC Berkeley in 2020. MMLU tests model performance across 57 subjects spanning STEM, the humanities, and the social sciences, and has become a near-universal fixture in model cards and capability leaderboards. Its appeal is understandable: it offers a standardised, reproducible measure of whether a model commands the factual and reasoning content that education requires. A model that cannot correctly answer questions about cell biology or the causes of the First World War is unlikely to be a reliable tutor in those subjects.

Yet education researchers have consistently noted a critical limitation of MMLU and benchmarks like it: they measure knowledge coverage, not pedagogical quality. Knowing something and being able to teach it are distinct competencies. A model that scores at the 90th percentile on MMLU may still explain concepts in ways that are developmentally inappropriate, fail to detect a student's misconception, offer feedback that discourages rather than scaffolds, or present information in a sequence that undermines rather than builds understanding. None of these failures would register on a knowledge benchmark. The gap between "what the model knows" and "how well the model teaches" remains largely unmeasured by existing standardised tools.

This gap has prompted efforts to develop more pedagogically oriented evaluation frameworks, though these remain emergent rather than established. Researchers have proposed rubrics drawn from learning science — assessing whether AI responses demonstrate awareness of prior knowledge, whether they use worked examples appropriately, whether they prompt metacognitive reflection — but no single framework has achieved the consensus or adoption that MMLU enjoys for knowledge testing. The field is, in this respect, still in a pre-standardisation phase, with individual research groups and edtech developers applying heterogeneous criteria that make cross-system comparison difficult.

On the safety and harm-prevention side, evaluation is somewhat more developed, though still contested. Red-teaming exercises — in which human evaluators systematically attempt to elicit harmful, inappropriate, or misleading outputs — have become a standard part of pre-deployment testing for large language models, including those used in educational contexts. Organisations such as Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google DeepMind publish safety evaluations as part of their model documentation, and third-party auditors have begun to apply structured adversarial testing to educational AI products. However, the scope and rigour of these audits vary considerably, and there is no independent regulatory body with the authority to certify educational AI systems as safe in the way that, say, pharmaceutical regulators certify drugs. Safety evaluation in this domain remains largely voluntary and self-reported.

Bias auditing presents a similarly mixed picture. Established techniques from algorithmic fairness research — measuring differential performance across demographic groups, testing for stereotyped associations in generated text, examining whether feedback quality varies by the perceived identity of the student — have been applied to educational AI systems with some regularity. Studies have found, for instance, that some AI writing feedback tools perform less accurately on text written in African American Vernacular English, and that AI-generated examples and illustrations default to culturally specific assumptions that may alienate learners from non-Western contexts. These findings are reasonably well-documented. What remains contested is how to weigh and remediate such disparities: whether demographic parity in output quality is the right target, how to handle trade-offs between fairness criteria, and whether bias audits conducted at the model level adequately capture harms that emerge only in specific deployment contexts.

A further structural problem is that most existing benchmarks and audits evaluate AI models in isolation, rather than as they function within actual educational environments. A model's behaviour when integrated into a learning management system, shaped by institutional prompts, and used by students under time pressure may differ substantially from its behaviour in a controlled evaluation setting. Ecological validity — the degree to which evaluation conditions resemble real use — is a recognised weakness across the field.

What emerges from this survey is a clear asymmetry: the technical infrastructure for measuring factual knowledge is relatively mature, while the tools for evaluating pedagogical quality, contextual safety, and equitable impact remain fragmented and underdeveloped. Closing that gap is less a technical challenge than an institutional one, requiring collaboration between learning scientists, AI developers, independent auditors, and the educators and students who bear the consequences of getting it wrong.

Chapter 12. Chapter 12: Frontiers — Emerging Territories

How are multimodal and voice-based AI changing learning beyond text — in languages, arts, sciences, and physical skills?

## Beyond the Printed Word: How Multimodal and Voice-Based AI Are Reshaping Learning

For most of its history, educational technology has been a text-bound enterprise — screens filled with words, exercises answered by typing, feedback delivered in written sentences. The emergence of voice-based and multimodal artificial intelligence represents a genuine departure from that tradition, extending the reach of intelligent tutoring into domains where language alone was never sufficient: the spoken word, the visual image, the physical gesture, the laboratory specimen, the musical phrase. The changes are uneven across disciplines, and many claims remain contested, but the directional shift is clear enough to warrant serious examination.

The most thoroughly documented transformation is occurring in second-language acquisition. Automated Speech Recognition (ASR) systems and conversational AI tutors — deployed through platforms such as Duolingo's voice features and Google's Speak — are giving learners on-demand access to pronunciation feedback and conversational practice that previously required a human interlocutor. Research published in Language Learning & Technology has examined how this changes the affective conditions of language learning as much as its mechanics. The anxiety that many learners experience when speaking to a native speaker or a teacher is measurably reduced when the interlocutor is perceived as a machine; errors feel less socially costly, and repetition feels less embarrassing. The result is greater practice volume, which correlates with improved fluency and phonological accuracy. This is an established finding, replicated across multiple studies and language pairs. What remains more contested is whether AI-mediated conversation develops the pragmatic and sociolinguistic competencies — knowing when to be formal, how to hedge, how to read silence — that emerge most naturally from interaction with human speakers embedded in real social contexts. The evidence here is thinner, and researchers are appropriately cautious.

In the visual arts and design, multimodal AI introduces a different kind of pedagogical possibility. Systems capable of generating, analysing, and annotating images allow students to receive feedback on composition, colour relationships, and spatial organisation in ways that text-based critique cannot fully capture. An AI that can overlay a compositional grid on a student's painting, or highlight tonal inconsistencies by rendering them in false colour, is doing something qualitatively different from a written comment. Early studies in art education suggest that this kind of visual feedback accelerates the development of formal technical skills. The more philosophically fraught question — whether AI engagement enriches or impoverishes the development of aesthetic judgment and creative voice — remains genuinely open. Some researchers argue that rapid AI feedback encourages iterative experimentation; others worry that it nudges students toward optimising for legible, AI-recognisable qualities at the expense of more idiosyncratic expression.

The sciences present perhaps the most straightforwardly promising case. Multimodal AI can analyse microscopy images, identify specimens, annotate anatomical diagrams, and walk students through the interpretation of spectroscopic data — tasks that previously required either expensive laboratory time or a specialist present in the room. In biology education, AI image-recognition tools have been used to help students identify cell types and pathological features with feedback that is immediate and specific. In chemistry and physics, systems that can interpret hand-drawn diagrams and equations extend intelligent tutoring beyond the typed input that earlier systems required. These applications address a genuine bottleneck: laboratory and practical science education has always been resource-intensive, and AI that can partially substitute for expert human observation during practice exercises has real equity implications, particularly for under-resourced institutions.

Physical and embodied skills represent the frontier where multimodal AI is most experimental and where the evidence base is thinnest. Computer vision systems can analyse movement — a swimmer's stroke, a surgeon's incision technique, a musician's bowing arm — and generate corrective feedback based on comparison with expert models. Proof-of-concept studies in sports science and surgical training have shown that such systems can identify technical errors that human coaches miss or lack the time to address individually. Music education offers a particularly interesting case: AI systems can now listen to a student's performance, identify intonation problems, rhythmic inconsistencies, and tonal quality issues, and provide feedback that was previously available only from a skilled teacher with a good ear. Whether this feedback translates into durable skill improvement, and how it interacts with the motivational and relational dimensions of instrumental teaching, are questions that longitudinal research has only begun to address.

Across all these domains, a structural tension persists. Multimodal AI is most effective at providing feedback on the measurable, the classifiable, and the technically specifiable. It is weakest precisely where human expertise is most irreplaceable: in cultivating judgment, taste, interpretive depth, and the capacity to work in conditions of genuine ambiguity. The technology is expanding the boundaries of what automated instruction can do, but it is not yet clear that it is expanding the boundaries of what learners can ultimately become.

What roles are autonomous AI agents beginning to play in education — as persistent tutors, course designers, or student proxies — and what new questions do they raise?

## Autonomous AI Agents in Education: Tutors, Designers, and Proxies

The arrival of large language model-based agents in educational settings marks something qualitatively different from earlier generations of adaptive software. Where previous systems followed rigid decision trees or statistical models trained on narrow datasets, contemporary autonomous agents can sustain open-ended dialogue, remember prior interactions, adjust their pedagogical approach in real time, and, in some configurations, act on behalf of users across multiple platforms. Education is encountering these systems not as a single tool but as a cluster of overlapping roles — persistent tutor, curriculum architect, and, most controversially, student proxy — each carrying its own promise and its own set of unresolved questions.

The most empirically grounded of these roles is the persistent AI tutor. Research on systems such as Khanmigo, Khan Academy's LLM-based tutoring agent, has demonstrated measurable gains in student performance, particularly in mathematics, when the agent maintains conversational memory across sessions and adapts its responses to a learner's specific pattern of errors. The persistence is not incidental: it is precisely the capacity to recall that a student consistently misapplies the distributive property, or loses confidence when word problems become multi-step, that allows the agent to intervene with targeted rather than generic support. These findings represent some of the more robust evidence currently available, though important caveats apply. Gains have been most reliably documented in procedural domains — arithmetic, algebra, structured writing — where correctness is easier to assess and feedback loops are tighter. Whether persistent AI tutors produce comparable benefits in subjects requiring interpretive judgment, historical reasoning, or creative synthesis remains an open and genuinely contested question. There is also an unresolved debate about dependency: some researchers argue that students who receive highly responsive AI support develop metacognitive skills more slowly, because the agent resolves confusion before the productive struggle that consolidates understanding can occur.

The role of AI as course designer is less empirically settled but rapidly expanding in practice. Institutions and individual instructors are deploying agents to generate syllabi, scaffold assignment sequences, produce formative assessments, and even recommend curricular adjustments based on aggregated student performance data. The appeal is obvious — curriculum design is time-intensive, and the bottleneck between pedagogical intention and classroom implementation is real. Yet the research here is still largely observational and practitioner-reported rather than experimentally controlled. What is clear is that AI-generated curricula tend to reflect the biases and gaps present in the training data on which the underlying models were built, which raises particular concerns about representation in history, literature, and social science. A course on twentieth-century literature designed with heavy reliance on an AI agent may systematically underweight non-Western traditions not because of any deliberate choice but because those traditions are underrepresented in the corpora the model learned from. This is not a hypothetical risk; it is a structural feature of how these systems work, and it demands active human oversight rather than passive trust.

The most philosophically charged role is that of the student proxy — an agent that completes assignments, participates in discussion forums, or sits examinations on a student's behalf. This is, in one sense, simply a technologically sophisticated form of academic dishonesty, and institutions have responded with detection tools, revised assessment designs, and updated honor codes. But the phenomenon raises questions that extend beyond integrity policy. When a student submits AI-generated work, what exactly has been learned, and by whom? The transaction exposes a tension that was always latent in credentialed education: the gap between demonstrated performance and actual competence. AI proxies make that gap visible and exploitable at scale, forcing a reckoning with what assessments are actually measuring. Some researchers argue this pressure is ultimately productive, compelling educators to design tasks that require genuine presence — oral defenses, iterative portfolios with documented revision histories, collaborative projects with observable process — rather than polished final artifacts that any sufficiently capable agent could produce.

Cutting across all three roles is a set of questions about autonomy, accountability, and the nature of the educational relationship itself. When an AI tutor makes a pedagogical decision — choosing to push a student harder or to offer reassurance — who is responsible for that decision and its consequences? When an AI agent designs a course, whose intellectual authority does the curriculum carry? These are not merely administrative questions. They touch on what education is understood to be: a transmission of knowledge, a cultivation of judgment, a social process of formation. Autonomous agents are capable of simulating many features of that process with impressive fidelity, but simulation and instantiation are not the same thing, and the difference matters enormously for how these tools should be governed, evaluated, and — where appropriate — constrained.

What is known and unknown about AI in early childhood education, where developmental stakes are highest?

## AI in Early Childhood Education: What Is Known, What Remains Uncertain

Developmental psychology has long established that the first eight years of life constitute a sensitive period of exceptional neural plasticity. During this window, the foundational capacities that underpin all subsequent learning — language acquisition, executive function, social-emotional regulation, and the formation of secure attachment — are being actively constructed. It is precisely because these processes are so formative, and so vulnerable to disruption, that the introduction of AI-mediated tools into early childhood settings demands a level of scrutiny that exceeds what is warranted for older learners. The stakes are not merely pedagogical; they are developmental in the deepest sense.

What makes this domain particularly difficult to assess is that the research base is simultaneously urgent and thin. Early childhood education has historically been under-resourced as a field of empirical inquiry, and the rapid deployment of AI tools into nurseries, preschools, and the early primary years has outpaced the accumulation of rigorous longitudinal evidence. Much of what is currently known derives from studies of screen-based media more broadly, from developmental neuroscience that predates AI-specific applications, and from a small but growing body of work on adaptive learning platforms designed for young children. Drawing confident conclusions from this patchwork requires care.

What can be said with reasonable confidence is that young children learn most effectively through embodied, relational, and play-based interaction. The developmental science here is robust: language acquisition, for instance, is strongly tied to contingent, responsive human communication — the kind of turn-taking, joint attention, and emotional attunement that characterises interaction between a child and a caregiver or skilled educator. AI systems, however sophisticated their natural language capabilities, do not replicate this relational texture. A conversational AI can respond to a child's utterance, but it cannot notice the slight hesitation that signals confusion, modulate its warmth in response to distress, or provide the co-regulatory support that helps a young child manage frustration. These are not incidental features of good early education; they are central to it.

There is also established evidence that excessive or poorly structured screen time in early childhood is associated with delays in language development and reduced quality of sleep, though the causal mechanisms remain debated and the effects are moderated by content quality and co-viewing with adults. Whether AI-driven interactive tools produce different outcomes from passive screen consumption is a genuinely open question. Some researchers argue that the interactive and adaptive character of AI applications — their capacity to respond to a child's input rather than simply broadcast content — may mitigate some of the concerns associated with passive media. Others contend that interactivity with a machine is categorically different from interactivity with a person, and that conflating the two risks underestimating the substitution effect when AI time displaces human-child interaction.

This tension sits at the heart of the current debate. Proponents of AI in early childhood settings point to potential benefits in contexts of genuine resource scarcity: where educator-to-child ratios are poor, where children have limited access to language-rich environments at home, or where children with developmental differences might benefit from patient, repeatable, low-pressure practice. Adaptive phonics and early numeracy tools have shown some promising results in controlled studies, particularly for children who arrive at school with weaker foundational skills. The concern is not that these tools are without any value, but that the conditions under which they help, and the conditions under which they may harm, are not yet well understood.

The data privacy dimension adds a further layer of complexity that is especially acute for this age group. Young children cannot meaningfully consent to data collection, and the behavioural and biometric data generated by AI learning platforms — including voice recordings, interaction patterns, and inferred emotional states — are both commercially valuable and potentially sensitive in ways that extend far into a child's future. Regulatory frameworks in most jurisdictions have not kept pace with the technical capabilities of these systems.

What remains most conspicuously unknown is the longitudinal picture. The children who are today growing up with AI-mediated learning tools as a routine feature of their early education are the first generation to do so at scale. Whether early and sustained exposure shapes cognitive development, social behaviour, or the capacity for sustained attention in ways that differ from previous generations is a question that cannot yet be answered, because the cohorts have not been followed long enough and the controls are imperfect. This is not a counsel of despair, but it is a reason for genuine epistemic humility.

The responsible position, given what is known and what is not, is neither wholesale adoption nor reflexive rejection. It is the insistence that AI tools in early childhood settings be held to a higher evidential standard than those designed for older learners, that their deployment be accompanied by sustained human presence rather than substituting for it, and that the research community treat longitudinal study of this population as a matter of some urgency.

How is AI reshaping special education and individualized support plans in practice, beyond its general accessibility promise?

## AI in Special Education: Promise, Practice, and the Limits of Automation

Special education has long operated under a paradox: the students who most require individualized attention are served by a system chronically short of time, staff, and resources. Artificial intelligence has entered this space with considerable fanfare, promising to resolve the paradox by automating administrative labor and personalizing instruction at scale. The reality emerging from practice and early research is more complicated — genuinely useful in some respects, troubling in others, and still largely unresolved in the ways that matter most.

The most visible application is AI-assisted drafting of Individualized Education Programs, the legally mandated documents that govern the education of students with disabilities in the United States. Platforms such as Goalbook, IEP Online, and a growing number of GPT-based tools now offer to generate draft IEP goals, progress narratives, and accommodation language, with the explicit aim of reducing the administrative burden on special education teachers, who by some estimates spend between thirty and forty percent of their working hours on paperwork rather than instruction. The efficiency argument is not trivial: a profession already suffering severe shortages cannot afford to lose its practitioners to documentation fatigue.

Yet empirical scrutiny of these tools reveals serious limitations that complicate the efficiency case. Research published in Exceptional Children in 2023 found that AI-generated IEP goals frequently failed to meet the legal and professional standards required under the Individuals with Disabilities Education Act — goals were often too vague, insufficiently measurable, or poorly matched to a student's specific disability profile. This is not merely a technical shortcoming. An IEP is a legal contract as much as a pedagogical document, and goals that cannot withstand scrutiny expose schools to compliance risk while potentially denying students the precise, accountable support the law intends. The concern is compounded by what researchers have identified as a tendency toward generic language: AI systems trained on large corpora of existing IEPs may reproduce the bureaucratic boilerplate that special education reformers have spent decades trying to eliminate, rather than generating the genuinely individualized plans the name promises.

Equity concerns add another layer of complexity. There is preliminary evidence that AI tools perform less reliably for students with lower-incidence or more complex disabilities — those with multiple co-occurring conditions, rare syndromes, or profiles that are underrepresented in training data. If wealthier districts with more sophisticated implementation support use these tools as a starting point for expert revision, while under-resourced districts treat AI output as a finished product, the technology risks widening rather than closing the gap in IEP quality. This tension between efficiency and equity is not yet resolved in the literature, but it is sufficiently documented to warrant caution.

Beyond IEP generation, AI is reshaping special education through adaptive learning platforms and real-time behavioral and academic monitoring. Tools that adjust reading level, pacing, and modality in response to student performance data have shown genuine promise for students with learning disabilities such as dyslexia, where the ability to shift between text, audio, and visual representation without teacher intervention can meaningfully reduce frustration and increase engagement. The evidence base here, while still developing, is more encouraging than for automated IEP drafting, partly because the feedback loop is tighter and the stakes of any single algorithmic decision are lower.

Predictive analytics represent a more contested frontier. Some districts are experimenting with AI systems that flag students at risk of regression or crisis based on patterns in attendance, assessment, and behavioral data, with the intention of triggering earlier intervention. Proponents argue this addresses one of special education's most persistent failures — the lag between a student's emerging need and a formal response. Critics raise legitimate concerns about surveillance, about the accuracy of predictions for students whose profiles are already marginalized in training data, and about the risk that algorithmic flags will substitute for, rather than prompt, genuine human judgment.

What the evidence consistently underscores is that the relationship between AI and special education is not simply one of tool and user. Special education is a domain defined by legal obligation, professional expertise, family partnership, and the irreducible particularity of individual children. AI systems that treat IEP generation as a text-production problem, or behavioral support as a pattern-matching exercise, are likely to disappoint precisely because they misunderstand the nature of the task. The more defensible applications are those that position AI as a capable assistant to expert practitioners — reducing clerical load, surfacing data patterns, and expanding the range of instructional formats available — rather than as a replacement for the relational and professional judgment that effective special education requires.

The field is at an early and genuinely uncertain stage. The administrative promise is real; the accuracy and equity risks are also real; and the deeper question of whether AI can support the kind of individualization that special education law envisions, rather than merely simulating it, remains open.

How is AI transforming informal and out-of-school learning — self-teaching, homeschooling, communities of practice, and family learning?

## Learning Beyond the Classroom: How AI Is Reshaping Informal Education

For most of modern history, serious learning outside formal institutions required either exceptional self-discipline, access to expensive tutors, or membership in tight-knit communities of practice. AI is now disrupting all three of those constraints simultaneously, and the consequences for self-teaching, homeschooling, family learning, and communities of practice are only beginning to come into focus.

The most documented shift involves self-directed learning through large language model-based tutoring tools. Systems such as Khan Academy's Khanmigo, ChatGPT, and Claude have made on-demand, personalized explanation available to anyone with an internet connection. Research from the Stanford Graduate School of Education and independent analyses confirm that learners are using these tools to pursue subjects at their own pace, revisit concepts without embarrassment, and receive explanations calibrated to their existing knowledge — capabilities that were previously the exclusive province of one-to-one human tutoring. The significance of this should not be understated: the Socratic method, long considered the gold standard of individualized instruction, has historically been rationed by cost and geography. AI makes something functionally similar available at scale. Whether the simulation is genuinely equivalent to human mentorship remains an open and contested question, but the practical accessibility gains are well established.

Homeschooling families represent one of the clearest early adopter communities, and the evidence here is particularly striking. Surveys of homeschooling households in the United States show rapid uptake of AI tools for curriculum planning, subject-matter explanation, and adaptive practice. For parents who are confident in some subjects but not others — a near-universal condition — AI functions as a knowledgeable co-teacher available at any hour. A parent fluent in literature but uncertain about chemistry can now offer a rigorous science curriculum without outsourcing it entirely. This does not eliminate the need for human judgment in education, but it substantially lowers the threshold of subject-matter expertise required to homeschool effectively. The caveat worth noting is that homeschooling populations are not representative: they skew toward households with higher baseline resources and parental education, which means the benefits documented so far may not automatically extend to more disadvantaged informal learners.

Communities of practice — the networks of hobbyists, craftspeople, amateur scientists, and professional learners who teach one another outside institutional walls — are being transformed in subtler but equally significant ways. AI tools are accelerating the onboarding of novices into complex domains by providing immediate answers to the foundational questions that experts in a community often find tedious to repeat. A newcomer to amateur radio, historical bookbinding, or open-source software development can now arrive at community forums with a more sophisticated baseline understanding, having used AI to compress what might previously have taken months of lurking and asking basic questions. Some researchers have raised the concern that this could erode the social fabric of such communities — the slow apprenticeship process has historically been where relationships form and tacit knowledge transfers. Whether AI-accelerated entry enriches or thins community bonds is genuinely unresolved.

Family learning — the informal, often incidental education that happens between parents and children, or across generations — presents a more complicated picture. On one hand, AI tools are enabling what some researchers describe as "collaborative inquiry," where a child's question about dinosaurs or black holes or the French Revolution becomes a shared investigation rather than a parental admission of ignorance. Families report using AI to explore topics together, with the tool functioning less as an authority and more as a reference that both generations interrogate. On the other hand, there is a legitimate concern about the displacement of productive struggle. When a child can instantly resolve any confusion by querying an AI, the cognitive work of sitting with uncertainty — which developmental psychologists associate with deeper learning and resilience — may be bypassed. The evidence on this specific dynamic is still emerging, and it would be premature to draw firm conclusions.

Underlying all of these contexts is a tension that the research has not yet resolved: the difference between access and equity. AI tools are lowering barriers to informal learning in measurable ways, but the learners best positioned to exploit those tools are often those who already possess strong metacognitive skills, reliable internet access, and the cultural capital to evaluate AI outputs critically. A self-taught programmer in a well-resourced household and a first-generation learner navigating AI without guidance are both technically using the same tools, but their experiences and outcomes are likely to diverge considerably. Several researchers have flagged this as the central equity question for informal AI-assisted learning going forward.

What is clear is that the boundary between formal and informal education is becoming more porous. AI does not replace the social, credentialing, and developmental functions of schools, but it is demonstrably expanding what is possible outside them — and doing so faster than researchers, policymakers, or families themselves have fully reckoned with.

What weak signals today — in research labs, startups, and experimental schools — most plausibly indicate what AI in education will look like in ten years?

# Faint Signals, Large Consequences: What Today's Experiments Suggest About AI in Education in 2035

The most consequential shifts in education rarely announce themselves loudly. They begin in underfunded labs, in schools willing to tolerate failure, and in startups operating well outside mainstream institutional comfort. The current moment in artificial intelligence and education is no different. Several converging lines of experimental work — in affective computing, multimodal sensing, and adaptive system design — offer plausible, if still uncertain, glimpses of what learning environments might look like a decade from now.

The most technically ambitious signal comes from research programs at MIT, Stanford, and Carnegie Mellon, where AI tutoring systems are being rebuilt from the ground up to perceive learners as embodied, emotional beings rather than text-generating machines. MIT's affective computing lineage, initiated by Rosalind Picard and now considerably extended, has produced systems capable of reading facial action codes, tracking eye movement, and measuring galvanic skin response in real time. The goal is not surveillance for its own sake but pedagogical responsiveness: a system that can detect the precise moment a student tips from productive struggle into defeated confusion, and intervene accordingly — slowing down, reframing, or simply acknowledging the difficulty before it hardens into disengagement.

This matters because one of the most durable findings in educational psychology is that affect and cognition are inseparable. A student who is anxious cannot consolidate new information efficiently; a student who is bored will not encode it at all. Text-based AI tutors, however sophisticated their language models, are blind to these states. They respond to what students type, not to what students feel. The multimodal systems emerging from these labs attempt to close that gap. Whether they will do so reliably, ethically, and at scale within ten years remains genuinely open. The technical challenges are substantial — physiological signals are noisy, culturally variable, and easily confounded — and the ethical questions around continuous biometric monitoring of children are serious enough that regulatory friction alone could delay deployment well beyond the decade horizon.

Yet the direction of travel is clear, and it connects to a broader architectural shift visible across multiple research programs: the move from AI as a content-delivery mechanism toward AI as a dynamic model of the individual learner. This is sometimes called "learner modeling" in the academic literature, and its ambitions have grown considerably with the availability of large language models and richer sensor data. Rather than adapting only the difficulty of the next problem, next-generation systems aim to maintain a continuously updated representation of a student's conceptual state, motivational profile, and learning history — and to use that model to make decisions across timescales, from the next sentence to the next semester.

Experimental schools, particularly those operating under competency-based or mastery-learning frameworks, are already running early versions of this idea. In these environments, AI systems track not just whether a student answered correctly but how long they hesitated, which distractors they chose, and how their performance varies across contexts. The data density is orders of magnitude greater than what standardized testing captures, and the pedagogical implications are correspondingly richer. A student who consistently fails geometry problems involving rotation but succeeds at those involving reflection is not simply "bad at geometry" — they have a specific gap that a well-calibrated system can target precisely. This kind of granular diagnosis, currently available only in well-resourced experimental settings, is a plausible candidate for mainstream adoption within ten years, particularly as the cost of the underlying infrastructure continues to fall.

The startup ecosystem adds another layer of signal, though one that requires more interpretive caution. Companies working at the intersection of AI and credentialing are experimenting with continuous, portfolio-based assessment that could eventually displace or supplement traditional examinations. The logic is straightforward: if an AI system has observed thousands of hours of a student's work, it has far more evidence about their capabilities than a three-hour exam ever could. Several jurisdictions are already piloting AI-assisted credentialing in vocational education, where the competencies to be assessed are more tractable. Whether this extends to higher-order academic domains — where the constructs are fuzzier and the political stakes around credentials are higher — is contested.

What these signals share, taken together, is a common implication: the AI in education of 2035 will likely be far less visible as a discrete tool and far more embedded in the continuous fabric of learning environments. The tutor that senses frustration, the system that models conceptual gaps, the platform that assembles evidence of competence over time — these are not separate products but components of an integrated infrastructure. The open questions are not primarily technical. They concern governance: who controls the learner model, who can access the biometric data, and whether the optimization targets embedded in these systems reflect genuine educational values or merely measurable proxies for them. The signals are real. The destination depends on choices that have not yet been made.
